MAGNETS FOR HANDLING GLASS.

It has been proposed to use ele6tro-mag- nets for lifting and handling large panes of glass. This is accomplished by placing a piece of sheet iron under the glass, and ap- plying one or more electro-magnets on the upper fa-cis of the glass. The electro-magnets attract- the sheet iron, and thereby hold the glass suspended while moving.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

The Suffragists' Den enstrations.—Fine weather I /secured the first of the two great Suffragist demon- tar:tier a in Londen, viz., the procession and Albert I Hall meeting oqanised by the London Society for rneu's Suffrage, and several other hundred Associa- tier s. It was n tr,ca,o ration of tie Constitutioeal Suffragists headed by Lady Frances Balfour, Mrs. Henry I Fancett, Lady Perry Somerset, and other ardent and j life-lerg Stffrapists, who have always worked for I eerrens' political freedom. Many women well-Leona in scciol end professienal circles and not a few wear- ing academic comes and regalia were to be seen in the' precession, ehich cerni ered several thousands repre- sentative of all classes, Item &eters, eriters,actres.ses, atd nurses, and don estic Ft milts end factory girls. i With bands, and tanners flying, and there were nearly a thousand harmers altogether, the different sections I aroused coesidera hie interest and enthusiasm as they passed along the streets, and the organisers were well eatisfed with the success of their demonstration, the attitude of the public being, for the most part, empa- thetic. The splendidly organised pre cession of ten I Meteor:el won en was a new thing tor London, and it very evidently impressed the crowds that it was a, &rue rat ration of a winning cause. Most of the women; who base eaten priscn fare for the glory of the cause , took part in the processicn and meeting, but the militant suffragists held their own Hyde Park demon- Etration on Sunday, June 21st. Special trairs are to be ten to bring adherents from all parts of the country and it is expected that this great demonstration upon which over £IO,CCO has teen expended will he attended ty cter a qesrter of a milli( n people. The Anti-Suffrage Movernene—Hitharto the Buffra- gises have had things much their own way. but they will row have organised opposition in the field against them. The Anti-Suffrage Association, under the Countess of Jersey, Lady Haversham, Julia, Marchioness of Tw(edale, 'Mrs. Humphrey Ward rind other well- known women, has been formed to carry on a campaign in the country against the Suffragist propaganda, and it is proposed to hold a counter-demonstration to the one in Hyde Park organised by the militant Suffragists. Of course both sides claim to speak in a measure for the whole of womanhood, but there is little doubt that most of the enthusiasm will continue to be found in the vitnps of the Suffragists. There have been Anti- Suffrage Societies before, but they never had anything but a feeble existence, even in Victorian times, when it might be expected that they would meet with a larger measure of sympethy and support than they are likely to arouse nowadays. Girls' Edncation.—Dr. Elizal•eth Blackwell, now in her BSth year, was the pioneer in opening up the medical profession to women, and her Dame was mscribed on one of the banners carried in the Suffragists precession last Saturday. She ens born in Bristol, and emigrated to America with her father in 1532. There she graduated as Doctor of Medicine iu 18-10,some sixteen years before Mrs. Garrett Anderson, sister of Mrs. Henry Fawcett became qualified medical practioner in this country. Since those time girls' education has undergone vast changes, rind it is interesting to note that reforms in this direction are at last mating their appearance in Germany. In that country the " new woman "is still in a state of infancy. However well-educated a girl may be, she is taught that her proper sphere is to com- bine the duties of cook, housemaid, and nurse, and to line in subjection to her lord and master. Now it seems she is to be allowed after this year to n.atriculate at the Prussian Universities,a step towards the equality of the sexes, which has come as a considerable shock to German bachelors and husbands. Dearer Meat.—HollECß IVCs are accustomed to tinter).- Hens in the prices of food, but none effects the average ; household more acutely than dear meat, and the general , increase of a penny to twopence per pound is said to be but a preliminary to the higher prices we may expect to shortly have to pay the butchers.—lf that is the Care it will mean considerable hardship to the great bulk of the population. There are vast numbers of people, more so in England and Wales than Scotland 2nd Ireland who ruin their health in middle life by j eating too much meat. They wonld he very much I better if higher prices induced them to consume a lesser quantity, but those to ehom dear meat means mere cr less enforced vegetarianism, belong to the large class of persons who have not the means to indulge as a rule, in a large carnivorous appetite. -Various reasons are given for the increase in prices, some economic, sad some artificial, such DS the manipulations of the American beef trust. Expert opinion however is mostly agreed that higher prices have come to stay hut reduced consumption will pro- bably have some effect, especially if that large section of the public with the means to command a wido choice of foods, would resolve to do as far as possible without butchers' meat. -Of all fruits, the most

The Strawberry Season--s. is the home-grown strawberry for which ne now look on the afternoon tea table. Like everything else, the strawberry has not escaped miticism, and it is said to be bad for those pro-disposed to gout, rheu- matism, and other ailments, but very few are deterred by such warnings from en indulgence in this most luscious fruit. Fortunately the objection to the straw- berry do not apply to strawberry jam, which housewives will soon he busy making. For this purpose the berries should be picked just order ripe, and it is all the better i if the juice of crushed gooseberries or red currants is added to the boiling. it is no lire trying home jam- snaking without a proper preserving pan, and it should Foil, not merely simmer or bubble, vhich means a longer process, during vhich the jam is all the time losing flavour and colour. Pots or jars should be made Lot, the contents being poured in boiling up to the brim, and they should be tied down immediately. ; Borne-made jam should be kept in a wain' dry closet, end should never be stored in a cold place. Washing Gloves.—The washing gloves in reindeer ' and doeskin, are greatly to the fore in shopland. hut as so many of us have discovered that their period of utility frequently fails to extend beyond the first wash, they are less in demand than formerly. The best plan is to invest in a good quality washing glove, and then undertake the washing process ourselves. The mistake the majority make is to rinse these gloves in clear water. They should be well rubbed upon the hands in -warm soapy wafer, and then rinsed in another basin of soapy water. The clean hither will thus be left in the gloves, and when dry they will be soft and pliable, and hot break into the holes of which so many wearers complain. An Excellent Example.—No fashionable woman ' dreams of wearing a linen, cotton or muslin gown, a second time, until it has been ironed, thus affording an example lesser lights would do well to follow, for quite apart from the improved appearance the ironing process Imparts, it certainly staves off the dip into the tub, and the gown's days are thereby lengthened. There is no doubt that we should gain much from a more general use of the iron, many a voile or silk gown, cloth or serge tailor-made, being the better for a smooth out, and pressing about the seams, flounces and hems. A skirt board covered with an old clean blanket, a sleeve board similarly treated, a tiny iron for those parts a medium iron cannot reach, and a" goose "—a very deep very narrow, and very heavy iron—should be stocked by all girls and women who aspire to cool summer attire at little cost, and to making or even keeping their clothes in good condition all the year round. MADGE.

DRESS-FASHION-110ME.

WEDDING PRESZNT VICTIMS.

The most acute sufferers from our system of wedding presents, declares -Clare North in M. A. P., are in reality the bride and bride- groom. She instances the case of the bride upon whom everyone showers silver. Every bride, she says. receives a vast deal too much silver. The metal is cheap, and can be purchased in a variety of shapes and patterns; consequently rose-bowls and vases, sugar sifters and cream jugs are showered upon the unhappy pair. A recent bride received no fewer than thirty-seven silver boxes, a massive silver fish, for which no use could be found, thirteen silver ink-pots, forty-four silver photograph frames (mostly small), four sets of coffee-cups in silver stands, at least a hundred silver things such as candle- sticks, tiny clocks, cake - knives, silver-topped bottles, pepper-pots, brushes, and calendars, to say nothing of the necessary tea and coffee ser- vices, trays, entrée dishes, and other household silver. And this girl can only afford to keep a nook-general and a house-parlourmaid. What a world of trouble lies in front of her!

BMA= SXIIiT FOR. WASHING MATEDIALS.

The smart and remarkably pretty skirt pictured in the accompanying sketch is distinctly new in shape, and is particularly well suited to realisation in washing Materials, alpaca, or de- laine. Round the top of the skirt comes a minute hip-yoke which in front- develops into a box- pleat of considerable width. The edges of the yoke are finished by a line of machine-stitching, whicla is carried down the box-pleat on either

ekb for a distance of five or six inches, below which the pleat is released and hangs loosely to the bottom. On to this yoke the fulness of the skirt is set in a succession of short tucks, all of which aro firmly machined down to the depth of the box-pleat in front, and then released. Three tucks of graduated widths ornament the bottom "Of the skirt, and a group of similar but narrower tucks placed about the level of the knees com- plete this charming model.

THE LITTLE TIE.

All sorts of little and big ties and cravats are worn now, and the narrow ribbon or silk model with tassels or balls of silver or gold to finish it off is too popular to be suited to exclusive taste. But it i a variation of the usual idea, says the Queen, to wear it tied and with the ends pen- dent at the back insfead of under the chin. And the ribbon thus, passing plainly round the throat in front can bo secured with one of the new slides or sustain a jewel at the wearer's discra- tion. Sometimes the balls at the ends are of real stones; a pretty set of turquoise pave work completed a tie of this sort seen recently, and in front a larger ball to match hung from the rib- bon, which in this case hacl been knotted there to form a loop for the ball. Tassels of seed pearls or garnets look very pretty used thus, and a couple of mid-Victorian earrings with gold tassels falling from filigree balls had been utilised in this manner, the balls making a pair of hand- some hatpins.

WOMEN AND THE VOTE.

If "The National Women's Anti-Suffrage As- sociation" is to succeed, it must not wholly de- pend for support upon the efforts of members of the social standing of those who signed the cir- cular letter sent out the other day, declares " Miranda " in the Lady's Pictorial. The associ- ation must bring into its ranks 7„eal working women; women who have had some experience pf the seamy side of life; women who make no noise in the world, but bravely do the duty in which circumstances have placed them. Women such as these could tell us what effect for good or evil the " vote " might have upon their lives, and more especially whether they have reason to think that the lack of it has added to their trials.

A D.kINTY LITTLE FROCK.

Pictured in our sketch is a delightfully pretty little frock of rather new shape. This charming garment is extremely simple in design, but so summery and dainty in effect that it would serve perfectly well as a "best" frock for the next few months. The original was carried out in white spotted muslin of not too transnarent weave. Down the front. from .top to bottom of the dress, came a wide box-pleat, which at the neck was out in a little V and edged with a band of Irish

crochet. From this box-pleat turned back a shaped collar, which narrowed sharply towards the waist, and was edged all round by the Irish crochet. A band of the same crochet adorned the skirt, which was very full and stood out in most jaunty fashion. The little frock was completed by a folded waistband of forget-me-not blue satin ribbon, which was finished in front, on either side of the box-pleat, by a big soft rosette,

LINEN COSTUMES.

A novelty of this season much in demand for holiday attire, says London Opinion, is a smart little whito linen coat and skirt trimmed with coarse lace insertions, and worn with -a coarse lace blouse. These are very useful and practi- cable, as they are easily washed, and are very little trouble to get up. They bave also the hd- vantage of being quite cheap, lend are wonder- fully smart, and effective for both town and country wear.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

GALANTINE OF CALF'S HEAD.—Prepare, and boil the head until tender, then bone it, and lay it out flat on the table, skin downwards, after which sprinkle over it, in alternate layers, some finely-minced parsley, hard-boiled eggs cut in thin slices, veal forcemeat, and thinly-cut slices of lean pink ham, with a good seasoning of salt, pepper, lemon juice, and grated nutmeg between each layer; then roll up the head as tightly as possible, bind it firmly round and round with white tape, tie it in a buttered cloth, and braise, or stew, it very gently for three or four hours according to the size, after which take it up, drain well, and put to press between two dishes, with a heavy weight on the top, leaving it thus until quite cold and firm. When required for serving; remove the tape binding and brush the galantine over with two or three coats of glaze, letting each coating set before adding the next; then trim the galantine neatly by mininw a thin slice trom each end so as to display the prettily variegated interior, and garnish tastefully with a thick close border composed of picked parsley, cut lemon, and aspic jelly roughly chopped, and coloured a pretty shade in red.—Nark Lane Express. •

===== 0002601_19080620_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGERS OF WOOD-ALCOHOL.

A number of well-authenticated cases have been recorded from time to time of persons who have lost their eyesight from merely in- haling the vapours of wood-alcohol, as well as from its use externally in liniments. Painters arc especially subjected to the dan- gerous effect of this poison, and some of the , eases—referred to were those engaged in ; using shellac varnish, who had, -by continued 1 absorption, impaired their eyesight, and ' finally become totally blind. That methyl lalcohol (wood alcohol) is poisonous exter- nally as well as internally is no longer a de- batable subject, declares the 'Scientific -.American, and too much publicity of the fact cannot be given, in view of the fact that denatured spirit will soon be a reality, and presumably denatured with methyl alcohol, I imparting its poisonous principles to the j new commodity."

===== 0002601_19080620_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
PEARL FISHING AND X-RAYS.

A note in Nature calls attention to an in- teresting practical application of the X-rays. In 1001 Professor Dubois shewed that radio- graphs of the pearl-bearing shells could be taken shewing the pearls in situ. 'He sug- gested that this method might be of use in the pearl fisheries of Ceylon. The usual method of pearl fishing is a wasteful one. Only one pearl is found in 100 oysters, and only one in every 100 pearls is of commercial value. Hence every pearl costs 10,00 oysters. Mr. J. Solomon, electrical engineer, New York, has endeavoured to work the method from a commercial point of view. Some hun- dreds of shells are exposed at a time. Those which thew no signs of pearl formation are put back into their beds, while those contain- ing good pearls are opened. Those contain- ing small pearls are placed in conditions suitable‘ for their further growth.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
SATURN'S RINGS.

Saturn's rings, these unique appendages possessed by Saturn alone of all the planets, have long been the spZeial objects of study. Every fifteen years the broad band of the rings narrows to a thin line of light prac- tically invisible in the largest telescope. Three different relative positions of earth, sun, and Saturn bring about this state of things. But at these periods, when the rings should be practically invisible, • certain spots or knots of light come into view. These were first observed by Professors Barnard, in 1907, at the Yerkes Observatory. They may be ex- plained, says a Globe writer, by supposing that the sun shines through the rings. And this agrees with the view that the rings are formed of meteoric fragments revolvina round the planet. Professor Lowell contends that these knots of light chew that Saturn's rings are not flat, but of the nature of tores or flattened anchor rings. Others, however, think the phenomena observed are quite con- sistent with flat rings.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BRAIN AND TIIE WILL.

Impressions conveyed to the brain when awake excite ideas which are for the most part, in a healthy mind, subjected to the re- gulating influence of the will, and if they give rise to acts these are voluntary and rtstional. But when the influence of the will is suspended, and it ceases to direct the cur- rent of thought, the acts which such ideas thus produced may at once excite are often strikingly rational or altogether absurd. Such example may be seen Ai persons intoxi- cated by alcohol, chloroform, ether, or laughing gas. This condition is bit in the drunken Cassio when he is represented stum- bling on to his knees, and then, the attitude suggesting the idea, beginning to pray. So again we often observe sight, sound, or touch starting a current of ickas in one un- der the influence of ether or chloroform, and it is interestinr, to note that these im- pressions do not for the /most part evolve correct ideas of the real circumstances of the case, because they are unusual ones,. but they are interpreted according to the habitual circumstances in which they are ex- perienced.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
COPPER-CLAD STEEL.

M. Monnot, a French metallurgist, has in- vented and put into successful commercial operation a process for coating steel with copper of any desired thickness,. The great beauty of the process lies in the fact that the original .block or ingot of copper-clad steel can be rolled, drawn, and pressed without in the slightest degree disturbing the adhesion between the two metals, and without appre- ciably . altering the ratio of the thickness of the copper to that of the steel. In order to make copper-clad steel wires and sections an ingot of steel is placed in a bath of molten copper. A sleeve is also placed in the bath round the ingot, leaving the desired space for the copper sheathing. The ingot and sleeve, fitted at the bottom with an arrange- ment for retaining the molten copper sheath, is drawn out of the bath and allowed to cool. Special attention has to be paid to the tem- peratures at which the various operations are performed. The advantages of copper-clad steel are that it is very nearly non-corrodible under ordinary conditions, and that it pos- sesses greater strength in proportion to its weight than standard copper.

INSECT PESTS AND FUNGI OF EGYPT.

Mr. Walker Draper, Director of the Gov- ernment Gardens Delta Barrage, Egypt (who has made a special study of the " Coccidm," or scale insects of Egypt, which have hitherto received very little attention, and the habits of which are, in many cases, im- perfectly understood), has published an im- portant brochure on the "Injurious Scale In- sects and Mealy Bugs of Egypt, together with other insects, pests, and fungi," which will prove equally useful to planters and ar- boriculturists in all zones, temperate and tor- rid alike. In 1891, Mr. Draper tells us, seven species of " coccidte," or scale insects, were recorded in Ceylon; but three years later seventy-two distinct species were found. A few of the pests are of use, including the cochineal insect (Coccus cacti) and the :Luca insect, which furnishes the well-known pro- duct "lac " of commerce. Many, perhaps most, of these pests are found in all coun- tries, but in tropical and sub-tropical centres "the absence of a severe resting stage in winter creates- an almost endless succession of broods Of commie following one after another," causing it to be a matter of prime importance to take the matter in hand at once and eradicate, or at least check, the pests before they become unmanageable..

FIND THOUSTANDS STAY SO.

ALLEGED BETTING AT QUAY FOLD.

Important Witness Absent.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Thomas Drew, wire cleaner, of 31, Leicester-street, was sum- moned for frequenting Quay Fold, Bank Quay, on June 11th, for the purpose of betting. Mr. Steel said he appeared for the defendant, and he had an application to make, namely, that the ease be adjourned for a week, as two witnesses he wished to call were absent. One of thorn was a most important witness. This was a man named Ormson, in the employ of the London and North-Wrstern Railway Corn pany,at the signal-box at Quay Fold, near which place it wag alleged that the betting took place. He understood that Ormson would be there that morning but be had been in the service of the Company for a great number of years, nod he seemed to think that if he were called as a witness it would prejudice his position with the Company. If the case was adjourned for a. week he would subptene the witness and compel him to attend. The Chief Constable said he thought it was rather unfair for Mr. Steel to make such an application which had been sprung upon the police since it was patent he (the Chief Constable) was not offering objection to a remand in another case. After some discussion the Chief Constable withdrew his objection, and the case was adjourned for a week. The Chief Constable stated that there was another betting case, that of Page. He received a communica- tion from Mr. Knowles, solicitor, about this case on Saturday asking him to have the case adjourned. Ele (the Chief Constable) now asked the Bench to adjourn the case for a fortnight. The magistrates agreed.

St. B3rnab3s'

The Lord Bishop of Liverpool (Right R.v. D. Chavasse), preached at St. Butnabas' Church on Nioadiy evening at one of the ssrvices in celebration of the patronal festival. Taking for his text the words "Whom having not seen ye love" (i. Peter, 1-8) ho said St. Barnabas was not a great man, but he was some- thing better—he was a good man. He had not the eloquence or the ability of St. Paul, but, as he (the Bishop) pointed out seven years ago in that Church the key-note of his life was helpfulness. He went about helping others. The source and secret of his helpfulness lay In his love of God. He was always trying to help his fellow-creatures, and he did so because he loved God. The Bishop referred to the modern tendency to reverse the order of the great commandments and Ont love of one's neighbour before love of God. St. Barnrthas loved God first, and his love of God made him love his neighbour with a deeper, wiser, stronger and more patient love. God had given us a golden ladder by which we might rise to• love him better, and the chief wings were desire, faith. service and love of the creature. He rejoiced that God was teaching us to love Him better by all the sweet charities of home , life. The offertory was on behalf of the Day and Sunday School Funds.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church. Sunday School Anniversary.

The anniversary of the Sunday school in connection with the above church too,c place last Sunday. Two sermons were preached by the Rev. T. Howell Jones, of Rotherham and formerly% of this circuit. As is usual on such an occasion special anthems and hymns were rendered by the choir and children, all of which showed the adruiraiile way. in which the children bad been trained by the organist, Mr. Frank Hickman. The soloists were Mrs. A. Booth, Miss E.Taylor and Mr. 11. Plumpton. Miss Mabel Henshall was advertised to render a solo, but .owing to indisposition she was, unfortunately, unable to do so. In the morning the Rev. Howell Jones, preaching from the text " Oh, Lord God ! behold. I cannot speak; for lam a child." (Ist Jeremiah, 6th verse), said that the prophet Jeremiah had been called to the caves and difficulties of the ministry. The record told them, in the vivid way which the Old Testament had accustomed them, in God's call given to that pronhe; of his reluct- ance n responding to that call ; of the reason for that reluctance was finally overcome ; and how the man was equipped for that great and difficult task, which God had committed to him. The narrative was a very significant one for all who were called to any ministry. The first call of Jeremiah was in the days of Josiah, the son of Amon king of Judea, in the 'l3th year of his reign.- Tie (Mr. Jones) said that everyone of them had lived from all eternity in the heart and month of God, and there was no human life, that was an accident or a caprice. . .

To the afternoon a flower service was held, under the chairmsnship of Dr. J. Bennett. The congregation was addressed by the Rev. Thomas Miller, who said that flower services were of recent origin. Many objected to them because they were in some mystarions way connected with Paganism and Ritualism, but it was impossible to object to a flower service, es everything bright and beautiful should have a place in their service that day. Thtt flowers conveyed a message of God's love and care. They also represented the offering of ' their own Brea to-Jesus, when they were most beauti- ful, and breathed the richest fragranoe. Their Strionr said that if God made a fl over; which could not think., toil or spin, so beautiful, would He not much more clothe those who were able to work, speak, think and love?

At the evening service the Rev. T. How-ell Jones took for his text: " And who knoweth whether thou art come to the kingdom for such a time as this?" (4th Esther, latter part of 14th verse). He pointed out that throngh n series of events, all of which were strange and some of which were tragic, Esther was called upon to occupy the throne lately filled by Vashti. She was a remarkable Jewish maid, the neice and ward of Mordecai a Jew; but a capricious monarch had chosen her as his queen. Soon afterwards the record told them that as the result of the schemiugs of an Amalelcite knight, a 'man who harl risen to a posi- tion of great influence in the Kingdom of Persia. the life of the whole Jewish ' race wls placed in imminent peril. This men had so worked into the weakness of the King as to secure 1 a royal decree authorising the extermination of the entire Jewish race throughout the King's dominions. Mordecal saw at once that here was the hour for which Esther was born. Mete was the oppor- trinity;for which 6119 was placed in her exalted posi- tion, and he sent a message to the Q leen urging her to go at once into the King's preience, and plc.:d for the life of her race. Na one knew the canricions character of her lord and master better than glean Esther did, and it was the law of this part that no one was allowed to enter the royal presence without the expressed invitation of the Qteen, and to do so unless the King happened to be in a clement mood was to risk life itself, and she shrank from the peril to which she was called upon to expo:') herself. Thia was the message that Mordecai sent her in reply : "Think not with thyself, that thou shalt escape in the King's house more than all the Jews., For if thrm altogether boldest thy 0%308 at this time then shall their enlargement of deliverance arise to the Jews from another place ; hat then and thy father's house shall be destroyed and who knon-eth whether thou art come to the kingdom for such a time as this ?" Pressed by this message Esther ventured into the King's presence and obtained the life of her race, lie (gr. Jones) wanted them that night to consider with him the special nonsennettle of this incident as it was interpreted to Esther by Mordecai and his interpreta- tion of the conserptences of these events suggested one or two great principles, which he wanted to discuss for a short time. The first was that privilege always implied opportunities and there- fore responsibilities. She was called to the king—that was her privilege. She was called to the king " For such a time as this "—that was her respon- sibility. The privilege was a great one, so the respon- sibility was correspondingly great- ;wen into peril. She had to risk her life in the discharge of her respon- sibilities. For a time the young queen overlooked the class connection of those two things—privilege and responsibility. All of them were called to the King at such a time as this." Each of them-was called upon at some time or other to a great responsibility, and it remained with them to fulfil it, as Esther had fulfilled her's to her race. The collections, which were-in aid of the Sunday Ischool funds, amounted to over £3l.

MISSIONARY MOTOR TOUR.

Meeting at Bald-street Wesleyan Curci.

In connection with the motor tour which has been organized by the Wesleyan Methodist' Church to stimulate interest in foreign 'missions, a public meeting was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday afternoon. Mr. Frank A. liarris nresided over a fair rotendence and the deputation consisted of the Rev. J. Gregory Mantle (London. traveller in India, Korea, China and Japan), Rev. T. A. Turney (district secretary), and Mr.- St.cnley Sowton (Afission house, London). The CHAIRMAN said the members of the deputation were engaged in work of a considerable amount of pleasure and a little novelty and they were glad to have them in Warrington to advocate the great cause in whose interests they were gathered together. The was glad to preside because be MIS a great believer in foreign -missions. There were two things in connection with the Church in which he was a fires believer—Sunday Schools and foreign missions. The latter was essentially the work of the Christian Church, end any Church which neglected ft fell very far short of the reality of its true mission. This land of ours and its various churches were, he considered, under an obligation to carry the Gospel throughout the world. They ought to do it partly in return for what they had received. It rested upon every earnest and true believer in Jesus Christ to give what aid ho or she could to the came. Their Church had always shown a great interest Orford A.F-.C. PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica. LECTURES FOR TILE WINTER.

Possibly it may seem as if this is hardly the time for paragraphs under the above heading, and yet it is already becoming quite late enough for those who wish to pre- pare good lectures with good sots of slides, for the earlier part of the winter season. There are so many delays even after the negatives have been made. A travel lecture (and this is the class in which photo- gtaphers are particularly interested) is a thing that grows constantly, and even though the preparer begins with a carefully considered agenda, he will probably modify it many times, include sabjects which he at first intended to omit, and throt, out some of those which he began by regarding as amongst the certainties. TUE PREPARINC OF A LANTERN LECTURE.

There are various ways of preparing a lectu-e, and no doubt more than one method has distindt advantages. No method, however, is a thoroughly good one which does not include a careful preparation of synopsis before the worker begins making his slides. It is well to divide the subject into a number of heads, to decide roughly how much time and how many slides will be given to each portion, and then to make selection of the actual subjects. If the lecture is to be illustrated by one hundred slides, the process of merciless weeding is greatly helped if the subject can he got out into say :five divisions, because it is easier to reduce 30 or 40 slides down to 20 than it is to reduce 203 down to 100. OPENING AND CLOSING.

In preparing a lecture, mtny —e—sc—eile-o.t7mentbers of photograhic societies would be greatly helped if they realised the immense importance of a good start and a good finish. Too .often the lecturer begins in a rambling wy, and the speaker hopes that he my be able to grip his audience by the time that he is abont half way through. This method is usually quite fatal. In fact, many of the best public entertainers say that if you have not absolutely secured the attention of the rutdierKm within the first minute (some say 20 seconds) your show is doomed to failure. VARIETY AND BREAKS. • -

In any lecture Uhistrated by Iriten slides, and deal- ing with one subject there is the tendency to monotony, end everything possible should be done to give some distinct variety. This can be done by two mean, firstly, by grouping the pictires and the matter round distinct interesting heads, and secondly, by providing complete breaks or incidents (a hearty faugh is perhaps ahnut- the best incident that can Karmen) at severol different stages during the course of the talk. It is better to group subjects than to epirate them all along. For instance, in a lecture on Holland, where there are, say, one hundred landscepes, and twenty figure studies, it is probably best to arrange 53 ' that four or five of the figure sot lies come in a group before getting half way through * the subject, and that eight or ten came in another group. nearly at the flush, while the rest-are distributed with reasonable equality through the remaining time. Now- a-days, with the possibilities of autochromes, a few colour slides may with advantage be interjected hero and there, if you can be atml itelv sere that yen have light enough to project them brilliantly. For this purpose, however, there should be a supplementary lantern, with very powerful light, and with relatively long-focus lens to give a small picture. If the auto- chromes for projection can be on half-plate, using a lant4rn with a large condenser, so men% the better. autochromes a pleasant variety is obtained by using a few stained slides, or tinted cover glasses, if yon can be qiiite sure that the sobjects are s rah as benefit by this treatment. One or two sunshine pictures toned to a distinctly brilliant warm brawn also help to make good breaks. -- SECURING LECTURES.

Most photographic societies now-a-days either belong to the Affiliation or to one of the Federations, and borrow from neighbouring societies the lecturers that they cannot supply from amongst their own members. There is much to be said, however, in favour of having an occasional lecturer from outside, especially if the arrangement can he made so that a thoroughly plenty and well-treated literary or soientifla subject, by a man of national and world-wide repnle,can be takelita hand. In such a Case., with good organisation, and a charge to the public, it should be possible to arrange so that the members of the photographic society can have admis- sion free, and yet the returns shag show a fairly substantial profit to go to the funds of the society. Many secretaries do not know how to et in touch with such lecturers, but there are well recognised agencies, snob as Thomas's Lecture Agency of Inngltarn TIOUISP, At-Tindal-street, \ London, NNW., who issue illustrated books containing synopses of lectures and entertainments on almost all classes "of popular RubjAc ts. Many of these are illustrated' throughout by photography, and a number of the leoturers am men who sr 4 well-known by name to photographic societies, as well as to the literary or scientific societies, to which they more umally appear.

DESPAIRED OF CURING BOILS

Suffered Incessantly for a Year— They were Very Large and Pain was Dreadful—Could Hardly Bear Clothing—Health Much Impaired.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
WAS ABSOLUTELY CURED BY CUTICURA REMEDIES

"I have to thank Cuticura Reme- dies for completely curing me of one of the worst attacks of boils it is pos- sible to imagine. I suffered from boils incessantly for a year, and I was / i so unsuccessful n trying remedy after remedy that I began to despair of ever getting my system clear of this dis- tressing ailment. My shoulder and back were principally affected. Any one who has had a single boil knows how dreadfully painful boils are, so it will be evident how much I suffered when I tell you that I had four large ones. They would be a long time corn- ing up and so very painful all the time that I could hardly bear my clothes on. They were very large, one on my- back extending to the size of a five- shilling piece, while the smallest was as big as a sixpence. My health suffered much as the result of the attack and I got into a very run-down condition. After I had tried other means without any benefit, I read of a cure by Cuticura, and though I had not much faith, I thought I could not do any harm in trying Cuticura Soap. There is something peon-- THE PAN-ANGLI- ?lady impressive in the CAN CONGRESS. gathering together of a great host of delegates represent- ing all parts of an enormous and scattered community such as the Anglican Church. The great Congress now being held in London is the outcome not, however, of a desire for impressive. nese, or for the display of mere numbsrs. It is essentially a gathering of workers. From an quarters practically of the globe come the representatives of a great religious body, perhaps the greatest outside the Roman Catholic and the Greek Churches that the world knows. Each in his far-off diocese or parish is carrying on the work of the Church to the best of his ability under conditions peculiar to the country in which he labours, and often far enough removed in character from those to which his colleagues in this country are accustomed. But the experience of the mis- sionary Bishop and priest is of concern to the whole Church. Those at home may learn from it useful lessons of devotion and endurance, and the power of abiding hope, which may often be a substantial encouragement in their own work, different though it be in character ; while the visitors from far-off lands will go back to their dioceses after their brief visit buoyed up by the knowledge that their work is recognised, that it interests the great body of their fellow-Churchmen at home, and that when they appeal, as they must do at intervals for sympathy and support, they will not be appealing to those who are unacquainted with the difficulties and the necessities of tbeir situation. That is one aspect of the Pan-Anglican Congress, and perhaps it is the most important. Another aspect, and one which in these times of religious uncertainty may perhaps appeal to the popular mind, lies in the common difficulties with which Christianity all over the world is now con- tending. Great questions have arisen, striking often to the very root principles of the Christian faith. The Church of England at home has not as yet been thrown into any violent paroxysms by these questions. Certain leaders like Dr. Goan have, it is true, made themselves responsible for views which raised grave issues. But 83 far the English Church, apart from what 19 known as the ritual controversy, has been I spared the spectacle of a "heresy hunt." • In America the Anglican Chureb, not, indeed, very / powerful, and possessing but a small proportion of ; members compared with some other bodies, hss

bad to deal with a serious question of this kind. °2oe" Crapsey case" is fresh in the memory. Without discussing the right or wrong of that Prosecution, it may be said without fear of contra- diction that the Anglican Church will have gained . soatething from the present Congress if there be some agreement between the prelate responsible for the conduct of the organisation in dilsrent Par s of the world as to the procedure in such cases as may arise in the future. In the di's- . -cuasions upon the broader questions raised by what is known as the higher criticism, there should 433 much interest. Bishops and clergy whose lives ate passed away from the centres of civilization in Africa or elsewhere may have found themselves little out of totr2l-1 with tha trend of modern thought. Their attention may have been con- centrated upon the work in which they were engaged to the exclusion of questions such as excite attention in this country and in America. They will have gained in learning something more Of the movement of thought, and the influence Which it is having upon the Churches. The Congress has interest for all who are of the 'Christian religion, even though they may be 'opposed in practice and principle to the Anglican Church. The gathering together of 7,000 Christians, coloured as well as white, from the very sods of the earth is a spectacle which cannot fail to be rnoving,and the welcome to the Congress has Cone from Nonconformists equally with ChurchPec'Ple. Christianity, despite controversies which till d:sintegrate it, is making for unity. The forces which are opposed to it grow stronger day by day. Their methods become nacre varied and their successes more frequent. lc therefore, the Christian faith is to retain its hold on the masses in civilised countries it roust be by the union of those who profess it in common, and the determination to march forward with the one end in view of conquering the armies of Evil to which their religion has always opposed itself. The Anglican Church has never known what it is to find its path entirely smooth. It cannot hope to do so in the future, nor perhaps is it to be .desired, since ease was ever an enemy to effort. What is to be hoped is that those priests and 'laymen who have gathered in London will go forth

The public is not likely to THE REeire FOR lose its interest in the DIAMONDS. " diamond-maker!' T he manufacturer of diamonds, rubies, and sapphires has beguiled humanity in all ages. The long-awaited publication of a recipe, in connection with which a sum of £GO,OOO had bean " advanced," with the disappearance .of the clever chemist, has provided one of the first-rate sensations of the year. The group which etood round the magistrates' table in the Palais do Justice. while M. le Poittevin opened the safe and took from it the envelepe sealed with five huge red seals would have made a ' picture for the Paris Salon when those seals were broken and tbe recipe was read. The crux of manufacture was the application of pressure "to the lid of the crucible." One can hear the laughter of the experts. As Sir Julius Wernher, who : advanced the money, pointed out, there was ' no information as to the method to be employed in• cooling the crucible ! Neither was there ' the presence of the diamond-maker to amplify a too-succinct account or to demonstrate the pro- cess. M. Lemoine, however, Wrote apologising for his absence, " Many things," he explained retrospectively, had " prevented him from carrying out his experiment in quiet." He had not yet made any more diamonds. The detectives were already planning other inroads upon his quiet. On so laughable a pretension it is not often, even in a day of sanguine speculation, that so much money has been committed. Tee secret of the recipe is out, and the public's interest in the next development centres to-day with the inquiries for M. Len-oine.

A decision of peculiar - Nox-Tatanixo IN interest to Warrington was THE POTTERIE3. given by th 3 Potteries Stipendiary magistrate on 'Thursday, when he disqualified a Tarlatan Coun- cillor because."when the defendant was nominated for the Council he was engaged in a contract with it." The case was of a simple, but sotnewhat (Itiornmon character, inasmuch that though the Cour.cillor is disqualified he is not found g"tY of any moral terpitude. Briefly, the facts are that a Government return was required from Medical Officers with regard to the deaths of legitimate and illegitimate children tinder one year of age. The Tunstall Medical 'Officer was obliged to procure the returns from the registrar—who in this instance was a member of the Town Council—and the Cannon made him a payment of two guineas for supplying the statistics. The acceptance of this pay- /tient caused the Councillor to become disqualifiad. The decision illustrates how strictly the law views anything in the nature of ‘ trading for profit between a member of the „ 3uozil and the Corporation, unless under the , of a limited liability Company. This -mistrar-Councillor acted quite innocently and Iv" Personally blameless, yet he lost his seat. Oa "t_ll. 0 ether hand members of limited companies can I,lirough their companies trade to any extent with 3rPomtions of which they are also members. It Is quite obvious that the law requires amending 8" as to deal with the well-known abuses of this latter privilege. . _

The solution of Problem lies in the realisation by parents and trn'lm that trades offer much more remunerative I:lloYment than clerking, and that a full coffer biter all preferable to a black coat.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 20, 1908.

The other day, writes a correapOndent, I witnessed an incident illustrating that the tables are sometimes turned on cats preying on birds. In a garden on the other side of the road a pair of magpies were rearing thejr progeny in a nest of some years' standing. One of the nestlings having strayed prematurely from the parental home was promptly snapped up by an ever- vigilant tabby. The infant magpie naturally pro- tested, and pussy had no so.oner selected his supper- table than he was attacked in the front and rear by the screaming and excited parent birds. There was a terrible rumps, and the pecking attack was so vigorously delivered that puss, though at first inclined to see the matter through, was feign to resign his prize and make good his own escape. - * * * The first Garden City has already begun to receive its contingents of summer visitors, who are able to take stock of the progress made at Letchworth during the past year. There are now a population of 5,25, 907 houses built or in building, 12 factories, 10 work- shops, and 41 shops, and still the community remain without a house licensed for the sale of liquor. Land is being gradually taken up, and the next few months will see a further development. A recent local lecturer has diecovered an undue prevalence of the attenuated neck and small chin amongst the inhabitants as " signs of weak character " and "characteristics in profoundly mental types" ! Again, "There appear to be the elements of p *tidy° re-action against develop- ments positively Socialistic." Letchworth is in many ways an interesting experiment. * * * Every now and then some stray denizen of the deep makes the mistake of entering the broad estuary of the Mersey, and pursuing the stream upwards on the flood-tide. On Tuesday, a venturesome porpoise swam ttp as far as the Weir at Warrington. Some say it. was looking for Alderman Bennett in order to point out that there are worse things than the smoke- nuisance,the condition of the river being one of them. Un fortunately the Alderman was not at hand to receive the deputation. On Wednesday a second delegate appeared in the form of a huge grey seal which was inveigled into " Uobby's Lack " and was summarily slain. How this creature came to enter the Mersey is a puzzle, for there are no seals any- where nearer than Ireland. The fact that it was caught above the Weir is additionally remarkable. * * *

Some idea of the vastness of the Derby estates can be gained from a list given in the "Estates Magazine" some time ago. The home estate of Knowsley covers 27,9701. 3r. 11p. ; the Fylde estate, in the neighbour- hood of Preston and extending westward towards Lytham, embraces an area of 10,115 a. lr. 21p. of the best arable and grass land in the County Palatine ; in the neighbourhood of Bury, in East Lancashire, are another 14,111 acres, a proportion of which overlays' rich coalfields let on favourable leases. There are also 1,500 acres at Withslack, near Grange, and 544 acres at Adgarley, bringing the total agricultural acreage in Lancashire alone to 60,232. Lord, Derby's possessions further include estates in Kent and Surrey covering 8,480 a. 2r. 27p., and in the neighbourhood of Macclesfield, Cheshire, extending to 9,754 a. 2r. 33p.; the New- market estate, of 350 acres, and the Elope, Fliatshire, estate of 100 a. Sr. 27p. In addition Lord Derby is the happy possessor of an exceedingly wealthy urban property consisting of 1,182 acres in Bootle, Liverpool, Walton and Kirkale, the greater part of which is lot on building leases. The Liverpool property must have yielded large sums. Land alongside the Mersey from North Docks, Liverpool, to Seaforth flattery, and 1,182 acres of hinterland, all formerly agricultural land, have been sold or leased for dock and other purposes, and it is stated that this has proved " a veritable gold mine." In the lifetime of the late earl, too, the coal deposits in East Lancashire have been exploited and leased. Knowsley Park haa_a thirteen-mile boundary wall and eleven lodges.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[BY OCR LONDON CORRESPONDIMT.]

We are now in the thick of politics, as we are in the height of Midsummer. The work of our politicians at present is specially inspiring ; but it is an anomaly that it should be closest and hardest at the hottest season of the year, and one of the Westminster problems of the moment is how to moderate this Mid- summer madness. „

THE NATION'S TIME.. There must be—the Prime Minister has acknow- ledged it—a redistribution of the Nation's work in regard to the seasoas, a sunlight•saving scheme, so to speak ; but most pressing of all necessities is a saving of the Nation's time in Parliament. The experience of this week has shown that there is nothing to pre- vent a most important subject being well threshed out, in so far as its principles are concerned, in a two days' debate ; and the allocation of time to the remaining stages before they are begun is a business- like arrangement, far preferable (since we must have closure by compartments, as it is called) than the profligate and irritating manner of letting time be squandered first, and taking vain measures to recover it afterwards. Time at St. Stephen's is money—too often money wasted. Now it is to be money saved, if our businesslike Prime Minister has his way. It is to be Old Age Pensions for one thing, and it will be a good deal more by-and-by, especially if he is able to carry out the scheme he adumbrated "—that is a fashionable word in Parliament just now—at the National Liberal Club last Friday night—the scheme of carrying on unfinished Bills from one Parliament to another. Nobody who has not spent much of his time in Parliamentary precincts can imagine what a saving of the Nation's time that would mean ; for it would at once destroy one of the most powerful inducements to waste time, the hope that by delaying a Bill's progress it will at the end of the session, be killed in the annual "massacre of the innocents." PROGRESS OF THE PENSION SCHEME. The opposition to the Old Age Pension Bill was insignificant. It passed its second reading by the magnificant majority of 388, the minority who voted for an amendment on the joint proposal of Mr. Harold Cox from the Liberal benches and Lord Robert Cecil on the Tory side being just under thirty. So both members of this odd combination were left in some- thing not far short of splendid isolation by their own parties. The oddest thing of all was that .Mr. Balfour, the leader of the Opposition, did not vote at all. Nor did Mr. Lyttelton, the Colonial Secretary in Mr. Balfour's Government. The Prime Minister, who i always makes an effective opening in his speeches,wasl quick to discern that this was Mr. Balfour 's intention, land the moment the leader of the Opposition sat down made great fun of the fact that he had left the House without light on the question how he was going to vote. The leader of the Opposition, in other words, had omitted to lead. He left Mr. Asquith to tell them that he was going to run away. In a sense even the minority of 29 did that. The amendment for which they voted was not a clear negative, but only a suggestion that subsidy was an inferior method to insurance. There was no distinct issue, as Mr. Asquith said. How could there be, after all the Opposition has said about Old Age Pensions? When the amendment 'had been disposed of by this vast majority of 388, there was not a voice against the second reading itself. So the House of Commons has passed the principal of the Old Age Pension scheme unanimously. FAME FOR MR. HAROLD COX. - - -

- - - With this notable debate the name and fame of Mr. Harold Cox will be irrevocably linked up, not only because of the ability and the courage of his speech,' but because of the notice taken of him by the Prime Minister, who, while turning the laugh of the. House of Commons against him, at the same time held him up to admiration and respect, and compared him and his sturdy virtues to one of the most giftei and thoughtful statesmen of a previous generation..l It is no small compliment to Mr. Cox to be compared with the famous Robert Lowe, once,after Mr. Gladstone, Mr. Disraeli's keenest opponent. Those younger politicians who want to see what election fun meant in those days should look up the files of the news- papers to see how the gladiatorial contest of 874 was carried on between Mr. Gladstone and Mr. Lowe on the one hand, and Mr. Disraeli on the other. It was about that time that Mr. Disraeli made the famous attack on Mr. Lowe and his professorial mind, and accused him of black ingratitude, since it was he (Mr. Disraeli) who had made it possible for him to obtain a seat by giving London University a member. Mr. Asquith's tribute to Mr. Harold Cox's independence and debating power is a suggestion that if the isolated position which he takes up from time to time in the hope of stemming what he considers to be the Socialistic tide should lead to his being stranded at some General Election, some similar means should be found for preserving him, like Mr. Lowe in those old days, to Parliament.

Conft

THE CRAZE FOR GERMAN EXAMPLES. The great feature of Mr. Cox's, speech, and a frequent topic of the great debate, was the laudation of the German system. It is rather odd how Germany in these days is always either a Parliamentary bugbear or a shining example. The Ministers in this debate, but especially the Prime Minister, effectually disposed of the German example as regards Old Age Pensions. The Prime Minister described the German system as built on the pillars of Inquisition and Compulsion, and ruled it out emphatically on the ground that "you annot brigade the industry of this country." Bat while disposing of "the German model," Ministers hold out prospects of something better for England. I Mr. Haldane especially threw a flood of light on the previous declarations of tha Prime Minister and the Chancellor of the Exchequer as to the purely initia- tory character of this scheme. It is but part of a whole series of social reforms, the completion of which is awaiting the Report of the Poor Law Com- mission. It is not only old men and women of seventy that are to be looked after in the new era of considera- tion and of justice to the toilers of the State. But they are dealt with first in order to fulfil the pledge of the Government "to make a beginning." PROMISED MODIFICATIONS.

In committee on this great" beginning," Ministers arp going to meet as far as they can criticisms of details in their measure. The second-reading vote approving the principle of the Bill will probably be held by the Speaker as confining its scope definitely, , and ruling out all further attempts to alter its ' character as a non-universal and a non-contributory Old Age Pension scheme. Amendments to let in the paupers the Government will not accept, partly because they cannot within the limits of their means, but also because they will be dealt with in the sub- sequent revision of the Poor iLaw system. The Chancellor of the Exchequer has virtually committed himself to a consideration at least of a sliding scale affecting the limitation of income. If a man has twelve shillings a week from other sources he cannot claim a pension as the Bill stands. The man who has ten shillings a week can, so he will be three shillings better o[f than his neighbour, who has

perhaps been more thrifty. It is probable that the conditions will be equalised by giving the twelve- shilling man a three shilling pension. With regard to the good-behaviour test, the uneasiness of the Labour Members on this head is probably removed by the undertaking of the Government to make it clear in Committee that only the proved wastrel and loafer need fear this test. The shirker who deserves nothing better than the workhouse is not to bring dis- credit on the scheme by being put on the same level as the industrious, thrifty, or merely unfortunate workman. Otherwise, there will be no iuquision into the recipient's past. FREE TRADE FINANCE OF THE BILL.

- --- - The Pensions Bill is to be based on Free Trade finance, and to be made, with the approval happily of all the Labour representatives, as far as possible a buttress against the schemes of the Tariff Reformers. The Fiscal Reformers, so called, have been willing to wound the Bill for tnis reason, but yet afraid to strike. The Chancellor of the Exchequer, in a notably animated passage of a speech winch fo7 the most part was so pacificatory as to be called apologetic, brushed aside with some impatience the suggestion of Mr. Chaplin that the financial means for carrying out the old-age pension scheme had not been thought out by the Government. Did the Opposition think the Government such fools as not to have seen their way before embarking on a momentous enterprise, the failure of which would expose them to the scorn of the civilised world? Something like that was his tone. And similarly confident was the Prime Minister, who claims that the credit of the nation is restored, and that its resources will be sufficient. The inquisitive- ness of the Opposition to have these resources dis- closed is, in the circumstances, unreasonable. That is the Ministerial responsibility —the Chancellor of the Exchequer's legitimate secret. CHURCH AND STATE.

A. great many black coats and clei ical ties are seen nightly at present in the Hciuses of Parliament ; and there are other ways in which the great Pan-Anglican -ferenee, at present holding a series of big meetings at the great halls af London, are connected with politics, although in a non-party spirit, let it be hoped. This is necessarily so, for the State as well as the Church of England, and all Churches, is busy with social problems. At a meeting held in the Albert Hall on Tuesday night, vat ious speakers, among them Canon Scott Holland and Miss Tuckwell, in discussing "The Church and Human Society," brought out clearly the close connection which there ought to be at least between Church work and the kind of politico- social work in which at present Parliament is so busily engaged.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Manchester is awfully particular. First it refused Winston Churchill, and now it declines Maud Allan.

, * * * Of all the noses in the world to sniff for -nastiness Where no one else has discovered it, commend it to the official nose of lianchester.

* * Sixteen thousand, eight hundred and fifty-five passengers were carried on the Widnes Transpetter Bridge during the Whitsuntide holidays, and 1,759 bicycles crossed.

* * Besides bathing the youngsters of the slums free in the open-air baths, the Liverpool Baths Committee have now found it necessary to give attention to dis- infecting their clothing while they are in the bath. The wretched rags which some of these poor children wear may be a prolific means of spreading infection, and the course proposed by the Cotn-nittee is a wise one. They are asking the Health Committee to bear the cost of the disinfecting plant. * * * We complimented the Paying and Sewerage Com- mittee rather prematurely for repairing the pavement on Warrington Bridge. As soon as they found out that people were pleased they stopped. The repair, at present is only half done, and the bridge is still dangerous. Absolutely the worst piece oi pavement on one of our main roads, that we know, izr the wood- block pavement opposite St. James' Church. It is an open question whether there are more holes than blocks. The holes have it in point of size, without any question at all.

* * * Among the licences referred for compensation by the Cheshire Compensation Authority on Wednesday was that of the Millstone Inn, Runcorn. Superintendent Ennion informed the magistrates that he had been in Runcorn six years and had never seen a customer in the house. This must have been something like the proverbial "millstone." We wonder what the licence will be based upon, if the trade has been on anything like the scale the police superintendent suggests. Had the Millstone been a grocer's shop, its owners would soon have closed it, but a licensed house is worth keeping open now-a-days even if a customer never enters the doors.

* * * The police seem to be awakening to the prevalence of some of the minor kinds of vice in Warrington. On Monday they prosecuted eight boys and six men for street gambling, with the result that penalties were inflicted in every case and a severe magisterial warn- ing given to the defendants. It is very satisfactory to rind that the men in blue are on the track of the boy-gamblers Many of the latter are hardened yowlg wretches and need strict surveillance. We wish the police would turn their attention to cases of drunken- ness. There is more intoxication at night-time in Warrington than ever there was, and no effort seems to be made to check it. The Trauoways Committee could give valuable assistance if they made it a rule that no drunken person should be allowed on the cars. hey simply encourage drinking by providing the drunkard easy and safe conveyance home. MOreover they have a duty to those passengers who keep sober. and expect to travel in comfort and decency. * * No one can be more wise than destiny.— Tennyson. To the noble, ability is a merit —to the mean a defect.—Diane. What we have been, makes us what we are.—John Pletcher. Logical consequences are the scarecrows of foals and the beacons of wise meo.—///c.rly. There is no such thing as finding true happiness by searching for it directly. It must Coyne by the service, the love, and the happiness we give to others. —I?. W. Trine. Our doubts are traitors, And make us lose the god we oft might win by fearing to attempt —Shakespeare. The greatest hope of our world lies in the certainty of heroes being born into it. —Carlyle. Love well thy work, be truthful in the mart, And foes will praise thee if thy friends depart. —Eric Mackay.

* * * The news of the sudden death of the Earl of Derby was received with surprise and regret. Althoagh he served the State in various administrative offices in his earlier life —the Admiralty, the War Office, the Colonies, the Board of Trade, and was Governor- General of Canada from 1888 to 1893 - he had not for many years taken any prominent part in politics. The younger son of the fourteenth Earl of Derby, he showed few of the characteristics which gained for his father the familiar title of the "Rupert of debate," , and Ids voice was rarely beard in the House of Lords. But, though he lived the quiet life, he was tactful and business-like, and maintained with dignity the traditions of a great house. Our readers will recall with interest that Lord Derby visited Warrington on October 28th, 1902, and opened the Muncipal Technical School. He is succeeded by Lord Stanley of Preston, who was a familiar figure in the House of Commons for many years, and was Postmaster- General in the last Unionist Government.

• The latest " sensation " on the Southport show ground is the haunted castle. The outside of the show (writes a correspondent) is painted to represent a castle, with two dark doors for entrance and exit. Passing through the turnstile, you grope your way .up , a passage which winds up and down in Stygian I gloom. Unearthly shrieks resound from every side. Suddenly the floor drops a foot or so, and you get your first" thrill.'! Then the passage branches off quickly, and you knock your head against the wall (your second "thrill."). Scarcely have you recovered when the floor begins swaying violently to and fro (your third 1' thrill."). You stumble on in the darkness and stub your toes against the fluor, which has now suddenly risen. You pass along the meandering track, and are just regaining your equanimity when a terrific gust of air nearly Mows you elf your feet. Next yon step on a board which is whizzing rapidly away from you, and jump off it into another whirl- wind. By this time you have lost count of " thrills women are shrieking hysterically, hidden machinery rattles and whirs around ;you, and you are only too glad to see the faint glimmer of daylight which , heralds your escape.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES TRADE DEPRESSION.

Widnes is in a bad state owing to trade depression, end almost one-third of the working population are out of work or on short time. bilany years have elapsed since there was so much poverty in the town. The chemical trade is suffering from the depression. Messrs. Ctossages, Boltons, and several other works are busy. Two hundred out-of-works were entertained to a sub- stantial meal at the Alexandra Theatre at noon on Wednesday.

GALLANTRY IN SAYING LIFE.

Local Cases.

Tae Royal Humane Society has made the followin 'awards for gallantry : Testimonial to S. Ennion, son of Superintendent Ennion, Runcorn police, for his action on May 9th in ! plunging into 10ft. of stagnant wet sr in a pit at ; Clifton and saving a man who, in an attempt at stlicide, had thrown himself in. Testimonial to Thomas Spottiswood, Earlestown, I Newton-le-Willows, for saving two children from the canal there on May 2nd. j Testimonial to James Wilson, Albion-street, St. Helens, for his action in rescuing John Tunstall from the canal there on May 30th. Testimonial to Henry Holland, aged 14, Northwich, for saving his younger brother from the Dane, which was in high flood, on May 12th, this being the second j time he has saved his brother from the river.

LOCAL LICENCES EXTINGUISIIED-A DESERTED INN.

Cheshire Licensing Compensation Authority hold i ts principal meeting at Chester Castle on Wednesday, Mr. C. H. Booth presiding. The Clerk k Mr. R. atolls) reported that at a meeting of the committee that morning Mr. Booth was elected chairman, and Mr. H. C. Yates deputy-chairman for the ensuing year. The- full alehouse licence of the Star Inn,Weaverham, the licensee of which is Mr.Williana Wood, was referred for compensatien, as was also the beer licence of the Cornwall and Devonshire Arms, Church-street, Runcorn, no opposition being offered. SUDDEN DEATH THROUGH MUNN.

An inquest was held at St. Helens on Homily on George Luot, a glass-gatherer, of Stanhope-street, St. Helens, who died on Thursday afternoon last under mysterious circumstances. On Thursday he did not go to bed, but had three gills of beer, and went to bed in the afternoon. About half-past four his wife heard a fall in the bedroom, and found him frothing at the mouth. She put him in bed, and Dr. Davies was sent for, but the man was dead on his arrival.—The Coroner said that the doctor was told that deceased had drank some liniment out of a bottle, but the witness denied this.—Dr. Davies said he was called to the house and found Lunt dead. A messenger told him that Lunt had drunk some liniment, A post-mortem had been held, and death was due to syncope, owing to fatty degeneration of the heart and liver, caused by drink.— There was no evidence of poison having been taken' and the jury returned a verdict of " Death from natural causes."

HORSE.-THE RONAVNES AGAIN. _

William Ronayne, Garret Ronayne, John Murray, and i Harry Austin were summoned at Newton Sessions last ; week, for cruelty to a horse in Southwork-road,Newton on May 6th. Garret did not appear and the others pleaded not guilty.—Thomas Danphy, a lad from Uolborne, said the four were in a conveyance, and he saw the Rouaynes cruelly beating the horse with thick sticks (produced). They continued till the horse staggered across the road and fell down. After that Murray thrashed the horse with the breeching strap, and the fourth man kicked it. They could not make it get up, but he thought they were a quarter of an hour beating it. A policeman came up, and they all went away, The horse straggled and kicked a shoe off, but could not get up. 'the defendants were not sober. I—John1—John Robinson and Joseph Beim, two other Golborne lads, who were with the last witnesss, confirmed his ' evidence.—John Unsworth, postman, of Lowton, also corroborated.—P.C. Hughes said he had information from Unsworth, and went and found the horse lying down, and in a shocking condition, bruised and bleed- ing, and blood and hair lying about.—William Ronayne said the horse was vicious, and lay down to rest atter kicking and getting its leg over the shaft. —The Bench considered it a had case ; they found the two Ronaynes and Murray 20s. and costs each, and Austin ss. and costs' and thanked the Inspector for bringing the case forward.. EARLESTOWN FOOIBALL CLUB. A POOR SEASON.

The annual meeting of the above club was held in the Go-operative Hall, Earlestown on Thursday night last week and the President (Mr. Tom Siddeley) had a , thankless task to induce people to take odice. The club has not had a very successful season, as, although starting with a balance of £l9 at the end of the year, £l7 in debts were still unpaid. The chief reason was a fall of £lOO in gate receipts. Mr. Siddeley said he did not know what they would have done but for Ma. P. Mel:Lardy who had got in about £4O in two seasons. . _

Mr. C. Wyatt said the Secretary's salary was £lO, but he had only got 24 on account. De, at any rate, ought to have his salary, as he worked to the club as cheaply as anyone. Mr. Perks said he was perfectly satisfied seeing that the balance was on the wrong side Mbar, hear). Mr. Forrester thought they ought to try and make members, who took out cards pay for them; he believed a lot of money was still outstanding. The Chairman said there was about VI

The Secretary said they were bound to get a few defaulters in 300 members, and the me i who took out 100 cards and had two defaulters did more good than he who soli a dozen which were all paid for. Mr. Appleton wanted to know why the committee did not carry out their resolution to make members pay. The Chairman said that was Committee business and ought not to be spoken of outside. For himself, he knew nothing of it, but a man who would not pay for his ticket was not a sportsman at all, lie felt strongly on this matter and offered to pay legal charges to com- pel men who were earning .C. 2 or .£3 a week to carry out their bargain, lint the Committee would nut allow him to go so far. _ . _ _ _

Mr. IVicHardy contended the bad Iveather had much to do with the loss on the season's working, and the Committee could not be blamed for the adverse balance.

Oa the motion of Mr. Ogden the accounts were passed. Mr. Ogden moved the re-election of Mr. Siddeley as President, which was carried unanimously. Mr. B. Ashcroft was re-elected Treasurer, Mr. A. Perks, Secretary, and Messrs. J. H. Alderson and C. F. Porter, Auditors. _ _ _

About 50 names were proposed for the Committee, most of whom declined to stand, but eventually the following were elected :—Messrs. T. Topping, P. McHardy, J. Sankey, J. Horridge, Robinson' Jolly, Allwood, W. Houghton, W. Harrison, Isaac E. Clough, N. Stephens, Lockett, Lilley and Hesketh. In his review the secretary poiuted out that their weakness in the past season was among t`le forwards who did not seem to get goals. The chief goal scorers were Lowe 8, Hughes 7, Bartley 6, Woods 4, and Fletcher 3. They had retained all their old players and would have Salt to assist them when repuired. They had signed a new man in Brosn, trona St. Helens Recreation, who came with good credentials, and hoped to get another good for- ward shortly. The spectators were always crying out for local lads, and so IOJUSi e were, they that one day Woods nearly left the field on account of their conduct. If he had done so the club would have lost £75, yet it was the general opinion he should come out. Forwards were hard to get and when they had got them it was harder still to improve on them unless they could pay him salaries and the Committee could nat see their way to so wllea they were .C6O in debt. Anyhow local lads did not let them down and one of them scored more goals during February than anyone else during tho whole season.

A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

Plumber Sues Builder. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will K.C., Thomas Hughes, master plumber, Knutsford-road, sued Emmanuel Davies. builder, Fletcher-etreet, for £ll 3s. 93. balance alleged to he duo for work done to defendant's orders. - Defendant paid £l5 into Court, with a denial of liability. -Mr. E. A. White appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Proctor, barrister (instructed by Messrs. Robert Davies and Co) represented the defendant.—Plaintiff's case was that he undertook to do work at two houses in Fletcher-street for £79 ort the understanding that anything else was to be pabl for as extras. Ultimately a dispute arose between the parties as to whether certain other work was included in the contract and plaintiff withdrew his men from the job, the present proceedings resulting, Defendant had paid him £5O, but plaintiff held he was entitled to the balance of the .f.,79, as well as to £lB Bs. 93. for extras, less £3 Sc. for work he admitted was not done. After a hearing lasting several hours, his Honour held that the contract covered all the work and that plaintiff was therefore entitled to £l3 10s. As defendant had paid £l5 into Court, his Honour directed that the balance of 30s. should be returned to - him and that he should have costs subsequent to the Payment into Court.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD

INQUIRY.

A 'Ratepayer's Criticisms.

Mr. W. R. North, M.lnst. C.E., an inspector of the Local Government Board, held an inquiry at the Warrington Town Hall on Wednesday morning into the application of the Corporation for sanction to borrow £469 for the erection of public conveniences at the %tarket Hall. Amongst those present were the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst), Mr. Robert Isherwood, and ICorporation officials. Mr. W. H. Woodcock (assistant overseer) submitted the usual statistics as to the population and financial position of the borough. He said a penny in the .Z on the borough rate would produce £1,045 and .on the improvement rate £943. The Town Clerk said there was a great need for sanitary conveniences in the borough, particularly for the use of ladies. There was no such place in existence at present except one below the market,which was used mainly by market tenants and customers. It was there- fore proposed to construct a new one in the Market Hall which was in the centre of the town. It was further intended to remove the existing urinal adjoin- ing the Market Hall, which was an eyesore and an obstruction of the footpath as well as an annoyance to the people in the neighbourhood. In answer to the Inspector the Town Clerk said the Market Hall was built about 1557 at a cost of £20,000. The hall itself was of a very little use now and only brought in a revenue of about .e 5 a week. It would have to be demolished before many years were over. Mr. Woodcock said there was a very small debt on the whole of the market but there was none specifically on the bell.

The Town Clerk said the building was badly con— structed ir the first place. . . .

The Borough Su'rveyor then expirined the plans. He stated that the successful contractor bad withdrawn his tender that morning and the next lowest tender Was £2l more. The person who offered to do the work for that extra amount had now telephoned that he required .£l7 more. The Town Clerk : We shall advertise again. The Inspector : That would be the safest course. Mr. Robert Isherwood said he represented the Property Owners' and Ratepayers' Association. They did not wish the Corporation to understand that they were in opposition to them in any shape or form or that they opposed the present application. They were anxious at all times to help the Corporation to abate nuisances but the Corporation was not always anxious to help them. A short time ago attention was called to the urinal at the end of Bewsey-street which he contended was in a worse condition than those adjoining the market ball, but because the property belonged to a certain ratepayer The Inspector: I must rule you out of order. Yon must not go into all that. I want you to speak to this particular scheme. Mr. Isherwood: We quite approve of it. The Inspector : I am not here to go into the relations between the Ratepayers' Association and the Corporation Mr. Isherwood: I think the ratepayers ought to be consulted in some degree and when such nuisances, condemned by the Medical o:licer of Health and other medical officers and by the Chairman of the Health Committee, are brought before the Corporation some notice ought to be taken of them. However, it was out-voted. It was rumoured when the vote was taken

....- that it was my property. The Town Clerk: I never heard a word about it.. This gentleman in.ppens to be the owner of property near it and he naturally objects. The inquiry then closed.
