WARRINGTON A TIED-TOWN."

" Clerical Sinners Against Their

Flocks."

The following appears in the Bortonwood Parish Magazine, published by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell (vicar.) " Warrington is not the only Tied-town, nor is it the greatest of brewers' towns, but it does not lag far behind. Tied hand and foot to the powerful brewing firms of Peter Walker & Slns, and Greenall Whitley & Co., poor Warrington has never had a fair chance. Beer has dominated Church and Town, and has been as

plentiful—though not as cheap—as water. The sweet flower 'temperance' has found it clifflcult to thrive and flourish in such beer sodden soil and drink infected atmosphere. " The unexpected always happens," and all adverse circumstances notwithstanding, total abstinence is to-day a real aggressive power in Warrington, Beautiful Warrington will 'arrive' not through bricks and mortar and garden cities, but by the way of sobrietry, social righteousness, and civi moralitr. The beer-ridden town of Warrington, delivered from the domination of the brewer monopolists, would prove , to be something more than a nine days' wonder, yet the ' day will inevitably dawn when the influence of these men will be shattered and sundered,and the local brewing houses languish for lack of employment. Speed the day ! All must help it on. Forward be our watchword.' Report has it that unhappy Warrington is the Town of Clerical penitents.' Would that it were s! There is need of penitence, or Clerical penitence, and it cannot manifest itself too soon. We must be forgiven if for the present, we bide amongst the unbeliev- ing Thomases.' Seeing in this case is believing, nothing short of seeing can persuade, convince. The Warrington Clergy to a man deserted the Bishop of Liverpool in his Licensing Bill Crusade, and absented themselves from the great Citizens' Meeting in the Parr Hall, whither they had flocked a few days previously to swell the brewers' platform. When the Clergy, who have hitherto acted as brewers' hench- men, appear on the anti-brewer platform their repent- ance may be believed and accepted. Short of this there can be no true repentance. If in earnest these clerical sinners against their flocks will come forward and publicly confess before their fellow-townsmen,— We have sinned in that we have .supported the Drink Traflio and begged the brewers' dollars. From this time forth we beg no more. The Bill whicls. before-time we have opposed we now most vigorously support.' Such a confession of error would exalt the Church in the eyes of the whole town whilst giving its death blow, and quietus for ever, to brewer domina- tion. He's a slave ' says Lowell, ' that dare not be in the right.' Temperance reformers in the Tied-town have wrought wonderfully, notwithstanding their work has been carried on under the most dispiriting and adverse rionditions. Although many a time hard pressed in the unequal fight, they have all along stoutly refused to surrender the Total Abstinence flag. The Hag * floats high to-day. The Warrington Band of Hope Union has made, and still is making', a splendid fight for the children of the Tied-town. ''This Union, which now embraces no less than twenty-six Band of Elope Societies, as recently as May last, held a delightful and crowded two days' i Festival n the Parr Hall. The organizers of this rather gigantic function are to be heartily commended for their pluck, complimented for their energy, and congratulated on their success. The late Mr. R. A. Naylor was, we remember,presideat for some years of this vigorous and enterprising Union. The Union owes much of its present day vitality to Mr. Naylor's never-failing sympathy and encouragement. The presidential chair now is filled, both fittingly and ably, by a lady, Mrs. James Dodds, wife of the doughty champion of the Non-Trading movement. Mrs. ()Ads is highly popular in Temperance circles, and is always ready with her adsom in response to the frequent calls made upon her time and powers."

Punctually at half-past eight Mr. Alfred Dunn Miniver entered the breakfast-room of the Cosmopolitan Hotel, and nervously adjusting his pince-ucz took a timid survey of the apartment. He was a youngish, middle-aged man, dark and slight, with a prematurely bald place at the back of his head, and very near-sighted. An enjoyable -entertainment was given by the juniors and cadets of No. 2 Corps, under the direction of Captain Charles Harper, assisted by Miss C. Hunter, at the Barracks in West-street, on Monday evening. There was a large attendance. Songs, solos, recitations, duets, &e.„ were well rendered, and the flag drill and the dialogue, " Welcome " were loudly applauded.

At the Police Court on Thursday, John Davies. of no settled abode, pleaded guilty to wilfullv breaking two panes of glass at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning, doing damage to the amount of 2s.—John Constable, porter at the Workhouse, said the prisoner asked to see the doctor with regard to one of his eyes. The doctor examined his eye, and was about to anply a dressing when prisoner broke two panes of glass, remarking that he would be treated in gaol.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for one month with hard labour. £l4 14s Good second-hand plano.--Woon,London rd.,Stockton Heath. Tunings and repairs.—Advt. Grand Duke in a Motor Smash. A serious motor-car accident occurred on Saturday evening near Shavington, a Cheshire village near Crewe. A large new car belonging to and containing the Grand Duke Michael of Russia :tad his wife, the Countess Torby, was being driven along the Newcastle-road when a child suddenly ran out and attempted to cross in front of the car. To avoid running over the child the chauffeur applied the brakes and turned into the hedge bank, with the result that the car crashed into a tree and was badly smashed. The Grand Duke and the Countess were severely shaken and were afterwards driven home. BABY CARRTAGES of every description.—J. S. GArtLAND,SI to 55,Bridge st.,Warrington.—Advt. A rag-gatherer named Sarah Ann Fast, Stoney-yard, Warrington, was charged at. the Borough Court, on Thursday, with stealing a quantity of cow's hair and a bag from the Vernon-street tannery, and also a flour sack, of the value of 2s , from a cart at Bridge Font. Superintendent Monaghan stated that Police- constable Clough was on duty in Hall-street on Wednesday morning when he saw the prisoner carry ing, a bag containing the hair. He questioned her and she said she had found it, but subsequently admitted having taken it from the yard at the Vernon-street tannery. The flour sack was also found in her nossel- sion, and she stated that she had found it at Bridge not, but it transpired that it had.been taken from the cart of Mr. Hallwood.—Prisoner was committed for a month. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Jan Moston, labourer, Crossley-street, and John Mills, ! hawker, Idaydock-street, were charged with haying been drunk and disorderly in Winivick-road, at, 5.25 on Saturday afternoon.and Moston was farther charged with assaulting P.C. Hayes whilst in the execution of his duty. - P.C. Hayes said the two prisoners were very drunk, and making use of bad language. They , refusal to go away and he was obliged to take .them into custody. Moston thereupon said " You are in for I, a warm time of it now." A tram car came up at the ' time, and the driver and conductor came to his assistance. After a great deal of struggling they managed to get them in the car. , When they got to Ithe Central Station, Moston kicked witness on the legs several times.—Thomas Crawford, the car conductor, !corroborated, and said prisoners uced bad language I Whilst they 'sere in the car.—The Chief Constable said that assaults upon the police were on the increase,and he hoped the Bench would look upon them in a serious light.—Moston was fined 20s. and costs or a month, , and Mills ris. and ccsts.—Crawford was thanked by the Chief Constable and the Mayor for the assistance he rendered the police.—Mills was then charged with stealing a barrel of potatoes and cabbages, valued at 129. 6d., the property of James Shamrock. The quarterly Council meeting of the Warrington Band of hope Union was held in Gregory's Cafd, Buttermarket-street, on Thursday evening, Mrs. James Dodds (president of the Union) presiding. The Assistant Secretary (Mr. H. Woolley) presented a brief report of the work done since the last meeting, including the May Festival which had been a great success, the visit of the delegates of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union, who had held their Council meeting in Wart ington at which a con- ference and two outdoor meetings were held, and the part taken by the Union in support of the Licensing Bill in Parr Hall in April last. The report also stated tLet the Union were arranging to hold a united meet- ing in Parr Hall on the evening of Temperance Sun- day in November next when it was expected to have the services of one the leading tempetance speakers of thtday. The report showed that the organization was justifying its existence by doing active work amongst the young. A paper was read by Jr J. Jarman on " Band of Hope work." In introducing his paper Mr.Jarman stated that he had been actively engager' in Band of Hope work for many years, and was glad to find the Warrington Band of Hops Union in such a flourishing condition. In the course of his paper he teferred to the Band of Hope like the Sunday school, being part cf the Church work, no Church organiza- tion being complete without its Band of Hope, as the success of the Christian Church depended very large!), upon its care for the rising generation.—A most interesting discussion followed, in which the following tsok part : —Messrs. Dale, Wright, Few, Muir, Whit- field, Wesley, and the President.— A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Jarman for his piper, and to Mrs. Dodds, on the natation of Mr. Clatwotthy, and seconded by Mr. Shaw.— A resolution ras passed supporting the Warrington Citizen's Temperance League in promoting an out-door demonstration in ro-t of the Licensing Bill. •

Personal. Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall were present at the horse show held at the latter end of last week at Richmond Park. Sir Gilbert was one of the judges in the harness classes. Accident in Bridge-street.

A singular acciTient occurred in Brigie-street at eleven o'clock on Saturday morning. Mrs. Banner (wife of Thomas Banner, postman, residing at 72, Longshaw- street) was passing alongside the building now ,in course of erection at the top of the thoroughfare, when a piece of timbor fell from the scaffolding and struck her on the head and knocked her down. She was at once picked up by P.C. Bennett who conveyed her to the Infirmary where she was attended as an in-patient.

Negligent Husband. At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, John Barry, Bakehouse-yard, was locked up at the instance of his wife, Mary Jane Barry, for arrears of mainten- ance due to her amounting to £2 14s. C3.—Defendant admitted the offence.—The Magistrates' Clerk said that on May 15th complainant obtained a separation order and defendant was ordered to pay his wife 10s. a week, and he had not paid anything at all.— Defendant was ordered to pay the money or go to gaol for a month.

He questioned whether he knew what day it was. He had Leen in the Workhouse but had come out again. He seemed to have a dread of it. He ms quite uncontrollable.—The Chairman (Dr. Fox) said prisoner would be sent to the Workhouse for 14 days, and the anthoritic I would deal with him as they thought fit. _ _ _

Robbery at the Town Rail. • A robbery was perpetrated at the Town Hall some time between eight o'clock on Saturday evening and five o'clock on Sunday morning. Mr. Walter Flood, chief inspector of nuisances, left his office at the time named on Saturday evening and locked up as usual. At an early hour on Sunday morning one of his assistants saw that the door was open, but, thinking Mr. Flood was inside, closed it and went away. On Sunday morning, at about ten o'clock, according to custom, Mr. Flood entered the office to open the letters when be found that several of the drawers bad been burst open. He made an exbaastive search and found that a sum of £l5 3s. had been stolen. Strange to say, a sum of money in another drawer was not touched. There had been no breaking of locks, but the door appeared to have been quietly opened by means of a skeleton key.

At the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday, before Dr. Fox and other magistrates, James Harris (17), 81, Winwick-road, was charged with stealing from a gas slot metsr, in the dwelling-bouye of his father, 84, Winwick-ioad, betwesn Wednesday, June 10th, and Saturday, June 13th, the sum of Bs. DI, the property of the Gas Company. He pleaded guilty.—Superin- tendent Monaghan said the prisoner and his eldest brother lived at 81, Winwick-road, and carried on the house. A short 'lime ago his father was sent to gaol for six months for neglect of children, and his mother was sent to a home for thrre years. A meter inspector, named George Higham, called at the house, and on examining the meter, found that the front portion had been broken, and tied up with pieces of wire. He examined the register, and found that the sum of Bs. 9d. was missing. The matter was reported to the police, and Detective Metcalfe and P.C. Clough went to the house and saw prisoner,when he denied all knowledge of the offence. Shortly aftsrward. how- ever, in the presence of his elder brother, he admitted it, and said: "1 broke into it on Wednesday morn- ing. I went to Manchester and sprit some of it, and I spent the rest in Warrington." Five years ago, prisoner was birched for stealing, and about twelve months ago, he was fined for gambling.—Prisoner was sentenced to three months' .imprisonment in the second division. TAIT.OR-lIADE CYCLE BREECHES from es. 11c1.— BAYns, Outfitter, Florsemarket-st.—Advt, P.S.A. Meetings. Mr. George Wallington presided at the weekly meeting of the Wycliffe Society, and extended a hearty welcome to the Rev. R. E. Letheren upon his first visit. The rev, gentleman took for his subject " The fine art of living." There were, he said, men who were simply drifters, or " anyhow men," whe greatly lacked the fine art. Then there were " jelly fish men " or men with no backbone, who tried to miss the difficulties of life. These also lacked the fine art of living. Then there were men who lived lives too much alike, who were determined to get on somehow or anyhow. These were also " lackers." The fine art of living was 's worthy conception. It was a cultivation. Just as the architect used his best thoughts, his highest Powers to make a splendid struc- ture, so the fine art of living required the finest and continued thoughts, to build up a manly character, so that life should not be spoiled, but made beautiful. Messrs. W. Beswi,ok and G. Colley also took part in the meeting. Mr. N. Cook sang ," When I survey the wondrous cross" and " Thou rt passing hence." Mr. W. 11. Payton presided at the organ.—There was a suroess- ful meeting of the Warrington Society in the Co-oper- ative Hall, Sankey-street. The meeting was chiefly composed of ladies, and was presided over by Mrs. Moore who commented upon the desirability of taking Christ as their pattern and leader. In all their actions they should try to practice the spirit of endur- ance, and so fulfil the law of Christ. Mrs. L?theren, of the Golborne-street Baptist Church, ''nave the appninted address, her subject being "llabies or Christian growth." She referred to Christians who required some kind of revival at short periods, but a true Christian felt a revival every morning he or she rose from repose. Two solos entitled " Nearer, my God, to Thee" and "San of my Soul" were ably rendered by Miss L. Plinston., Miss M. Plinston was the accompanist. ' Above all Wear BANKS'S HATS and CAPS.—Advt. Boilermakers Dinner.

A most enjoyable and successful dinner and social evening was held on Monday evening by the members of the Warrington branch of Boilermakers and Iron and Steel Shipbuilders Society. This took place at Atkinson's Cafe, Bridge-street, and was to mark the re-opening of the dispensation, which has been under- going several much needed improvements.—After dinner, Bro. T. Elwell was in the chair. The toast of the King and Queen and Royal Family was proposed by Bro. H. Aishen, and responded to by Bro. W. Carrick and the rest of the company, who very loyally and heartily joined in giving this toast musical honours. The second toast " our society proposed by Bro. Hill (district secretary), and suitably responded to by Bro. Higginson (district delegate), was also very heartily accorded. The chairman gave a shor address in which he remarked .on the growth and improvement of this branch of the society. He pointed I out that the branch was now almost entirely composed of intelligent young men, and naturally these ought to bring newer methods, and more enthusiasm, into the working of the branch, which would be all for the good of the society Bro. Higginson (D. Delegate), who had attended for this purpose, by the kind invitation of the branch,then opened the dispensation. remarking upon the pleasure it gave him to do so. He gave an address upon the growth of the society, and the consequent increased financial responsibilities incurred, which was listened to with great interest by all present. The rest of the evening was very pleasantly passed, several songs being rendered in excellent style by Bros. 11. Holt, H. Wadsworth, E. Lambe, A. Creswell and J. Catter with Mr. Pennington presiding at the piano. At the close Mr. and Mrs. Atkinson were warmly compli- mented on the splendid dinner provided and after votes of thanks had been passed to all who had helped to make the evening such a success the party separated after singing Auld Lang Syne," everyone declaring they had thoroughly enjoyed the evening.

MY D:

I hope ysitt have all had a very jolly time during the Whitsuntide holiday. Don't you agree with me that a day or two spent away from Work and lessons is an excellent arrangement? When I went to school I used to be delighted when the half-term holiday came round. I wonder if you get that now in your school life—a day's holiday in the middle of each term. You must remember that Ms many, many years since I went to. school—(you would believe me if once you saw me)—and sometimes the customs of school life alter. Well, my letter is short this week—that bothering printer man is worrying me for it, and won't let me write more now. So, with love, until next week, I UNCLE NED. I

THE RUDE CHILD.

There was once a rude child who would never say please, With arms on the table would sit at her ease, And call out to mother, with words such as

these: "Pass me the butter, give me the cheese." The fairies this, very rude child to tease, Once blew her away in a powerful breeze Over the mountains and over the seas. Then she came to a valley where never a dinner she sees, But down with the ants, the wasps and the bees In the valley she must live till she larns to say please. A. WRIGHT.

I once knew a man who lived in the midst of • a grove full of all kinds of trees. He was an old man, with a grey beard, blue, loving eyes, a gentle voice, and tender, winning ways that made him dear to all around. I say he was dear to all—bird and beast, man and woman, boy and girl—but the birds were his chief friends. When he first went to live among them, they were shy and timid, but they soon found, by the glance of his eye and the tons of his voice, that he liked to have them near, and would not hurt them. So day by day their trust in him grew stronger, until all the birds of the grove knew him, and woke him at dawn with their noisy chatter. Thus the love and trust of birds were a lifelong joy to this old man, and such love and trust no boy or girl can fail to win with the same sweet smile, and gentle voice, and tender, loving care for all created things. ALICE IRONS.

WHY IT WOULDN'T COOK.

At a gas cookery exhibition an old farmer entered, who,, having listened to the merits of gas cooking stoves, ordered and paid for a cooker, which was duly sent to his home. About two months after the same farmer called on the manager complaining that the gas cooker was a fraud, and that he had tried

- i 1 everything, and nothing would cook n the I "blooming" thing. The manager asked if any- thing were wrong with his gas, to which he re- plied, "Gas. What is gas?" ETHEL OWEN. 1 --1--- I ' .. AN OVERCOME DIFFICULTY.

"Ala, here it is!" said Ella, when her bright blue eyes espied, Upon her right, in Gloucester-street, a building tall end wide. "The top bell I was told to- ring "—and Ella gave a sigh— t, oh, dear me, I am afraid I cannot reach so high!" so it proved. though Ella stood on tiptoe, striving hard To make her fingers meet the knob, they missed . by half a yard. "I cannot do it," then she said, "so I—yes, I must stand And watch till someone tall comes by, and lends a helping hand." .No thought came into Ella's mind of going home to tell , That someone else must fetch the soup—she could not ring the bell. No, she had come for Mrs. Brown, a neighbour, , poor and ill; She found a way to keep her word because she had the will. With patient mind she waited till a lady „lame in sight, ' She then stepped forward eagerly, and asked in tones polite: "Please will, you ring the topmost bell? "—her voice was sweet and clear— The lady smiled, and said at once, "Of course, I. will, my dear l"• The bell was rung, a maid 'appeared, the jug was promptly filled, And Ella safely carried it—yes, not a drop was spilled; then she briskly ran to school With glad- ness in her mind— happy are the boys and girls who prac- tise being kind! girls and boys, true comforters, who never leave undone What they can do to aid and Cheer a sad or suffering one, Who bravely ask, as Ella did, for help in time of need To carry out a kind resolve to do a friendly deed. ; G. THE WOLF AND THE CAT.

One day a wolf ran out of the forest into a not to pay a visit but to save its life, for a pack'of hounds were after it. Seeing a cat on a fence, the wolf said, "Tell me, my friend, who is the kindest man in the village, that I may ask him to hide me from them." "Go quickly, and ask the smith to shelter you," said tha cat, "he is a kind-hearted man." "Quite true; only not long since I ate one of his sheep," "Well, then, you may try the baker." "I fear he is angry with me, too. I stole one of his kids." "Run across the street, then; the shepherd lives there." "The shepherd! I should be afraiat to meet him. Ever since the B:7ring he has sworn to kill me fcr killing a lamb." "Dear me, that is bad; but, perhaps, the butcher will save you." "I think not, for I killed one Of his calves." "Then you have made an enemy of every man in the village," sail the cat. "How can you hope for help line? No, they are not so foolish as to save you to their own hurt. And, after all, you have only yourself to blame. What you have sown, that you must now reap."

NORA. MOORING.

FREDDIE AND THE CHERRIES. Freddie saw some fine ripe cherries Hanging on a tree, And he said, "You pretty cherries, Will you not come down to me?" "Thank you kindly," said a cherry, "We would rather stay up here; If we ventured down this morning, You would eat us up I fear." One, the finest of the cherries, Dangled from a slender twig; "You are beautiful," said Freddie, "Red and ripe, and oh, how big!" "Catch me," said the cherry, " catch me, Little master, if you can"; "I would catch you soon," said Freddie, "If I were a grown-up man." Freddie jumped and tried to reach it, Standing high upon his toes; But the cherry -bobbed about And laughed, and tickled Freddie's nose. "Never mind," said little Freddie, "I shall have them when it's right," But a blackbird whistled boldly, "I shall eat theth all to-night." • Nome LILY MoomiNG. "I WANT TO GROW."

"Mother, I do want to grow,". said little Mary; "I do not like to be so small." One day her mother had been so busy that she did not miss tiny Mary, but when dinner was ready she went to look for her. Where do you think she had gone? Her mother opened the door, and found her little girl standing in the rain. "Come. in, dear," said mother, "you will ;take cold." "Please, don't be cross, mother,"/said Mary; "the rain makes the flowers grow, so 1 thought it would make me grow, too

D. AYRES.

AFTERNOON TEA. TEA. Of all the things that give me joy It is to ask a girl anti boy To come to tea with me. I have a tease pink and pearl; 'Twas mother's when a little girl, And now it's mine, you see. I have a table of my own— A present from my sister Joan— And silver spoons as well; Cook makes the cakes, I make the tea, And pour it out quite cleverly For Cousin Bob and Nell. Nell chats about her school and games; I tell her my new dollies' names, And all about their dress; But Bob sits eating all the while; He clears the plates, then, with a smile, He says, "It's scrumptious, Bess." JESSIE GOI7LI3OTIRNE. A WISE KING.

There once lived a wise king who it was said could answer any questions put to him. People from all parts of the world came to ask his advice if they were in any,trouble or difficulty. One day an old man who had heard reports of his great wisdom came to him and, bowing almost to the ground, said, May it please your majesty to ans(ver me a question?" "Say on, said the king. . _ .

The old man then said: "Can you tell me why,, futving two eyes and two ears I have only one mouth?" "Yes," said the king, "you have two eyes that you may see everything well, and two ears that you may hear everything well, but you have one mouth so that you may not talk too much." Linn RY4N.

IF THERE WERE NO FLOWERS.

I sometimes think what a dreary place,would be this earth of ours If God, when He made the trees and grilse, • had forgotten to make the flowers. How dull the fields and woods would be, bow bare the earth won:d look, . Were there no bluebells within the glade, no primroses by tho brook. 0, shouldn't we miss the lily's breath thld the fragrance of the rose, And the eight of the numberless wasencuns that daily open and close! .But I'm glad to know God didn't forget to give us His gift of flowers, That deck the hedgerow and strew the vales and bloom in the woodland •bowers. SAMUEL HOwEllill.

INL AND- COR R ESPOISD ENO E.

The term " Inland Correspondence " means cor- respondence posted within the United ICingdom, inlluding the Isle of Mari and the Channel islands. "Inland Corresponleace " is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) postcards (e) Parcels

(c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size.

LATTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follaws Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... Id. For every additional 2 A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a latter insaffiziently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth.

_ POST-MOS. Tho prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest p flcial post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 3i inokes in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 2 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and /-inch wide) bearing the address may now be Mixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. lIALFP3SIT PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post cm be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, &0., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method.

NEWSPAPER PoiT. The prepaid rate of postage on a rogistered news.. paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is J. Newspapers not registered at the General Pest o:lice aro treated in the pcst like any other printed matter.

PARCEL PoST. The rates of postage on parcels are as follows: For a parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 PP exceeding 1 lb. t, 2 lb. 0 4 „ ff • 2 lb. ft 3 lb. 0 5 PI IP 3 lb. f I 5 lb. 0 6 PP ft 5 lb. Pt 7 lb. 0 7 7 lb. „ 8 lb. 0 8 Pt If 8 lb. I, 9 lb. 0 9 PP PP 9 lb. tl 10 lb. 0 10 ft „ 10 lb. PI 11 lb. 011 No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The 81'3 allowed for an inland parcel in:— Greatest length... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combinei 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. 6in, in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. 61n. round its thiekeat part ; a short parcel may be thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft, in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. -Ths postage on a paroel must be prepaid,. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sander should see that the weight, sire sill postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (PORMIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, swept that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The castoms charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. BIONEX ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order fur more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a Id. can bo contained in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—'Tot exceeding £l, 21. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 41.; £2O, H. ; RD, ad. •, £4O, 101. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money crier can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. _ .

MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order i may be issued for places abroad varies n each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, 6d. • and above that, 31. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 61. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with coat of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order elice in the United Kingdom, and possess tho advantage oviir money orders that they can be cashed at any money order aloe. If crossed, they can be pitid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by ad., from ad. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are up to 21. 6d.; Id. up to 155.; 13d. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always till in the name of tho person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is I:limbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums.

Troit, (tNANn).

The Postmaster-General pOssesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of ed.,, which covers 12 words, and an additional .I,d. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the !Costal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, es far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be Kelm* Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is is.

rELEORAMS (FOREION AND COLONIAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE Sevrsos BAN.

These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the normal deposit must not exceed £5O, an I the total amount £209. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children unicr seven years of age cannot he withdrawn until thelchild reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a spacial form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the mount of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is el or over, a special acknowledgment is sent from the head London Oleo. Interest is allowed at the rate of 2} per cent. Any amount up to and including Li may be withdrawn 'Without previous notice' on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can be expedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPIIONE ANT) TRUNK LINE CHARGES. _ .

'there°,

Between the hours of 7 p.m. and 7 a.m., six minutes conversation is allowed for the same price as three minutes between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., and a reduced charge is made for three minute calls.

MOTOR CAR AND CITLE RRITILITIONS. Each one must be registerod with the council of a county or county borough, the foe being 21 for a motor car, and 59. for a motor cycle. A registered number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. , Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences. The highest speed at which a car may be driven on the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to the presence of the motor car on the road, the driver must supply his name and address, and the owner and number of the car, under heavy penal- ties. The driver must produce his licence at the request of a police constable.

The Examiner, Saturday June 20, 1908. PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

She: "So you have had a great many thril- ling escapes?" He: "Yes; I am still a bachelor."

"Why doesn't Walker stop to speak? I thought he knew you." Barkins : "He used to, but I introduced him to the girl he married. Neither of them recognises me now !" *

Ethel: "How long have the Nevilytiches been in society?" Bob: 'From the way they play golf, I should judge about two days."

Doctor :‘ "Are you troubled with cold feet?" Fair Patient: "Not now. My husband's off on a business trip."

Bacon: " She says she is twenty-eight years old." Egbert : "Well, she looks as if she would say she was about that old." * *

Tom: 'So you've been married a year! Now, say, Gus, honest Injun, does your wife greet you as warmly as she did at first?" Gus: "Warmly? She fires up every time I open my mouth,'

Cook: "What shall we have for supper?'' Boarding-house Keeper: "Not much of any- thing. The boarders won't be in condition to eat to-night. We had bakery mince pies for dinner."

* • Father (threateningly): " I've a great mind to thrash you!" Little Son: " Take a fellow of yer size. Take ma!" Father trembles at the thought, and sits down:

Miss Urban (from the city) : "Oh, there's no doubt that city life broadens a person's mind." Mr. Hayseed (who once spent a week in New York) : " Yes'm, it broadens the mind; but, Jehoshaphat, how it narrers the pocketbook-"

" De man dat nurses unreasonable hopes," said Uncle Eben, "may be foolish, but ain't so foolish as de one dat nurses vain regrets." * * • -

" I see, Katie, that New York is to have one policeman to everyss2l inhabitants," said the lady of the house. Well, ma'am, I've got mine," was Katie's reply.

"I would deem it a great favour if you would give me a little writeup," said the poet to the editor. "Nothing would please me more," re- plied the editor. "When do you contemplate dying?" • «

"It seems," said Citiman, "that Subbubs 'spends most of his time nowadays arranging Cook's excursions." "Nonsense! he's in the in- surance business still." "I know, but it keeps him busy taking new cooks out to, his place to spend a few days." •

'Jinks : "I called at your boarding-house to-day and saw the table nearly set for dinner. and I must say things looked very neat. I never saw a whiter table-cloth in my life.') Binke : "No,. she doesn't make her tea, coffee, or soups strong enough to stain."

Polite Doctor (cautiously) : "Your husband is suffering from overwork, or excessive indul- gence in alcoholic stimulants—it is—ahem—a little difficult to tell which." Anxious Wife: "Oh, it's overwork. Why, he can't even go to the theatre without rushing out half a dozen times to see his business partners."

"I wish," said Mrs. Oldcastle. "that I had Mrs. Waddington's savior-faire." "Yes," re- plied her hostess, carelessly tossing her 6000- dollar dog-collar on the dressing table, "I like it, too. I was looking at some downtown at Sel- lum and Sendum's the other day, but they didn't have any left that was anythini like hers." * *

The man lay prone upon the pavement. Blood trickled from a ragged gash in his throat, and formed in a little pool near his head. There were no marks of violence—nothing to show how the man had come by the accident. For a minute the great detective was baffled. Sud- denly, a strange light shone in his keen gray eyes, while a smile of satisfaction played about his lips. "Have you discovered how the man's throat was cut?" they asked. "Yes. He's wear- ing a collar that's been in the laundry twice." Mrs. Elite (in a five-thousand-dollar-a-year flat): "Oh, it's perfectly terrible the way things have gone to-day. That janitor has been just as contrary and disagreeable as it is possible for a human being to be." Miss Elite: "Did you bow to him deferentially this morning?" "Indeed, I did. • I always do. Did you?" "Yes, ma, I bowed to him most humbly." something is the matter. To-morrow morning try kissing him." * * " You say you wouldn't like to get out?" • exclaimed the prison visitor. "Wiy, I thought you were in for life." "Oh, worse than that, ma'am," replied the convict. " I'm in for a • double life. I've got two wives waiting out- side."

Puffing and blowing, the fat passenger began to climb to the upper berth in the sleeping-car. "Pretty hard work, isn't it?" said the man in the lower berth. "It is," answered the fat pas- senger, "for a man of my weight." "How much do you weigh, may I ask?' "Three hundred and cighty-seven pounds." "Hold on ! Take this one !' exclaimed the other, his hair begin- ning to rise on end. "I'd rather sleep in the upper berth, anyway. The ventilation is better."

Miss Tremont : " Since the only .way to under- standingly embark in the discumion is by prac- tical experiment, I will relinquish my individual freedom and genealogical cognomen to immolate my identity on the altar of metaphysical investi. cation."

BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE AMATEURS. INDIVIDUAL MERIT COMPETITION.

This was concluded on Monday at the Cemetery Green, Warrington. before a large attendance, and ended in a victory for T. Ellison, a member of the home green. Out of the sixteen left in for the fifth round fourteen were members of the Warrington clubs. There was nothing very surprising about the results in the fifth round, but in the sixth round a grand game was witnessed between Tom Glover and young Cleave, the game eventually en,ling in a win for Glover-21-18. In the semi-finals Ellison and Glover each won their games and met in the final. A keen and exciting game was witnessed, Ellison at length gaining the verdict by two points.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

-- NOTICE TO CORIZESTOADENTS. . Authors of letters intended for publication most in all (uses send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Walking Day. Sir,—Seeing our worthy Mayor has again issued his order forbidding any tratfic through the various streets the scholars parade, could I kindly appeal to those in authority and the public generally to keep a clear passage wide enough for the large banners to pass through. It is very trying to those in charge to see the crowd line up just to allow two scholars abreast to walk through, when there is sufficient room if they would keep nearer the footpaths. I trust we may have the hearty co-operation of all concerned for the comfort of the little ones who cannot help themselves.—Yours INTERESTED.

The Tariff Reform Llagne and Unemployment. Sir,—l don't intend to waste any more time and space in discussing oils and lamps when there ar3 matters of more importance and more relevant, especially as your correspondent does not pat any name over his oil and lamp shop. To the latter portion of Anti-Protectionist's letter which indicates a strange mixture of sympathy, prejudice and disjointed reasoning I should like, with your permission, to reply very briefly. Can he give us any evidence that Free Trade has prevented children having dirty faces, unkempt hair, ragged clothes and bare feet ? Has he forgotten what the late Prime Minister said about the large amount of poverty which existed, and what Mr. Lloyd- George said at the Colonial Conference, and that neither of them suggested a remedy. Tariff Reform is the most practical and the most honest remedy because it will make more work and provide money for social reforms without robbery and injustice. If we had all the money which foreigners have taken from us in taxes upon our manufactures' it would pro- vide a generous old age pension, but if Anti-Pro- tectionist is not also an irrational sentimentalist he knows that no kind of policy, fiscal or otherwise, will prevent children being neglected. Assuming from his language that he is a Socialist, will ha give me some proof that Socialists attend to their children better than other people? I don't want a reply consisting of a lot of rambling theories, such as Mr. Grayson gave me at Parr Hall. Socialists have been blowing bubbles long enough. Let us have something sensible for a change.—Y ours &c., CHRIMES. G 79, Knutsford-road, Latchford.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. A. E. Beck and the Nen-Trading

Issue.

Sir,—Mr. A. E. Beck's letter in your last issno intro" duces points that were replied to at frequent intervals during the Non-trading agitation. A company cannot trade with a Corporation of which one of its share- holders is a member. The law forbids it, and for excellent reasons. If the trading concern is a limited company, and the Councillor or Alder- man holds the hulk of the shares, the posi- tion is morally the same, though not legally. 'Warrington wisely decided to follow the moral issue. We had tried the line Mr. Back advocates, and found a needless—nay, a wasteful expenditure in bricks and mortar resulted. Members of the Council were floating Limited Companies to trade with the Corpora- tion in all sorts of commodities from whitewash to partridges. The rates were going up wildly and no one could quite explain why. Warrington made up its mind to stop the trading and did so. Mr. Beck now invites it to reverse its decision. His citation of the case of a judge goes against him. In the old days, Warrington Aldermen and Councillors regularly helped to decide their own contracts, and on one famous occasion an interested man opened a number of tenders when 00 otior member of the Council was Trcsent, and then sought to profit by the information obtained, before it became public property. Does Mr. Back include men who act thus in his category of "best men? Let him look nearer home. Since his letter was written a Councillor has been unseated at Tr:install because he was a contractor (as Registrar) with his Corporation to the extent of £2 2s. a year. Apparently if he could take his son into partnership and form a private Limited Company (under the new Company's Act) he could contract to the extent of £20,003 a year, and all would be well. Mr. Beck thinks so. So does Mr. Roberts. 'Warrington is wiser. The "beat men " are better out of temptation. Na man can be buyer and seller at the- same time and do his duty to both sides. Mr. Powell Williams helped to make that clear to Mr. Roberts and to Warrington six years ago, and we don't want to keep learning the same lessons over again. It is too expensive ! Yours &c.,

OLD GUARD.

Warrington, Jane 19th, 1008.

BRASS BANDS AT NEW BRIGHTON.

Crosfield's Win Third Prize.

The brass band contest annually promoted by the management of New Brighton Tower has invariably attracted widespread attention, and this year's event, held on Saturday, was as popular and as successful as any of its forerunners. Unfortunately, the weather was by no means favourable to a big attendance, but nevertheless, there was a surprisingly numerous assemblage of admirers of the various bands and of music lovers generally. Fifteen bands, representin,a the tiaras counties of Lancashire, Yorkshire, and Cshire, competed. They were as follows :—Rotherham Borough (conductor, Mr. Charles Elsom), Irlam, St. John's (Mr. Chris Smith), Irwell Springs, Black Dyke, Gossage's Soap Works, and Shaw (Mr. William Rimmer), Birkenhead Borough (Mr. J. G. Dobbing), Lindley and Foden's Band (Mr. A. Gray), Grosfield's Soap Works, Goodshaw, Horwich Old, and Pemberton ?Old (Mr. W. Halliwell), Norland (Mr. A. Owen), and Litherland (Mr. George Rogans). The entries originally numbered seventeen, but there were two absentees—Coppull Subscription and Raven- head. Handsome prizes had been offered. The first prize was the Grand Tower 50-Guinea Challenge Cup, in addition to £3O and a silver-mounted baton in case (presented by Hawkes and San, London), and the other prizes were :-2nd, £2O; 3rd, £l5; 4th, .212; 3th, 210 ; 6th, £3; 7th, £5. Crosfleld's Soap Works were the holders of the clap from last year. Mr. Richard Stead (Huddersfield), acted as adjudicator. A selection from Rossial's works constitated the test piece, and was admirably calculated to reveal the capabilities of the performers alike as to technique, tone, and expression. Commencing about two o'clock amid a discamforting downpour of rain, which did not cease until sortie three hours later, but which effectually -failed to damp the enthusiasm of the competitors or their followers, the contest lasted for about six hours, and it was after eight o'clock before the decision of the adjudicator was announced. Mr. Stead was confronted with a difficult task.' The playing genel ally attained an exceedingly high ' level of excellence, and at times it was very difficult to differentiate between the musicianly qualities of the competitors. Two or three bands, however, gave evidence of surpassing skill. The performance of Irwell Stings, under Mr. Riraraer's able conductorship, evoked the lion's share of the applause. It was an interpretation marked by splendi d and well-balanced tone, fine execution, and intelligent expression. The playing of the trombones, more especially in the con- cluding passages, was notably pleasing. Crosfleld's and Black Dyke also came in for spacial notice, although the majority of people were in thorough accord with the decision of the adjudicator in awarding first prize to Irwell Springs. The order of successes is as follows :-1, Irwell Springs ; 2, Black Dyke ; 3, Crosoeld's Soapworka ; 4, Shaw ; 5, Lindley ; 6, Goodshaw ; 7, Notland.

A RIVER SENSATION. -- Huge Seal Caught at Paddington Lock.

Following the appearance of a porpoise in the Mersey at Warrington on Tuesday, more excitement was created by the capAtre of a grey seal at Paddington Lock, better known as Ihbby's Lock, near Warrington, on Thursday morning. It is a 'remarkably fine specimen, being 8 feet long and 5 - feet round the chest, and weighing about 13owt. How the seal got into such uncongenial surroundings can only be conjectured. It is assumed,however, that it came up the river on a high tide and was carried over Latchford Weir. It unsuspectingly entered the lock where its presence was soon detected. The lock gate was promptly closed,and tho seal found itself in a trap. A gun shot soon put an end to its life, and it took the combined efforts of several men to haul the seal to the bank. rhe presence of such a splendid specimen i in the river is the more extraordinary when it is remembered that the gray seal is seldom seen at all GO these sandy coasts. It generally makes its home on the rocky coasts of tho West of Scotland and similar places. The capture was soon noised abroad and large .numbers of people visited the lock where the seal was placed on view at a small charge. We are glad to hear that the Museum Committee has now secured this interesting specimen and that it is to be mounted and will shortly find a place in the Warring- ton Museum.

TRADES UNIONISTS AND THE LABOUR PARTY.

THE PROPOSED WORKING ALLIANCE. -- 1 Messrs Vivian and Maddison Stand Oat.

VIGOROU3 MANIFESTO.

Mr. Henry Vivian, M.P., and Mr. F. Maddison, M.P., itave addressed the following letter to Mr. Charles Fonwick, M.P. Secretary of the Trades Union group in the House of COMIIIOII9. House of Commons, June 18, 1903. Dear Fenwick,—The announcement& in the Press yesterday, confirming the vote of the Trades Union group on the question of a working alliance with the Labour and Socialist party in the house, seems to make it desirable that we should explain why we cannot accept the decision arrived at. The first of the two resolutions passed, which pro- vides for joint meetings in the House from tune to time to discuss matters primarily affecting the wage- earners of the country, we heartily endorse. It is the second resolution we find it impossible to accept. This reads as follows :—" That the Labour party and the Trades Union group agree that their members shall in no way oppose either members of the Labour Party or Trades Union group or candidates endorsed by, the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Con- gress and by the executive of the Trades Union party." It will be seen that under this agreement existing mem- bers of the Trades Union group purchase their freedom from attack in their own constituency by the Labour and Socialist parties by agreeing never to oppose not only existing members of the Labour and Socialist parties, but also all candidates which the evecutive cares to- endorse in the future. Much as we dislike the Trades Union graup restricting its freedom, we were willing, as you know, for the sake of haru,ony, to favourbly consider restrictive proposals which were equitable and mutual, and when the question was first before us the Trades Union group unanimously endorsed our claim that the group should have some scope to increase its strength in the house by endors- ing candidates who should be as free from attack as existing members. The effect of the agreement, however, is practically to close the door to men coming into the house who desire to join the Trades Unions group, but it is kept wide open to any person, trade unionist or not, or to employers if they happen to be Socialists, if endorsed as provided in the resolution. Such a situation emphasises the evil of trades unions being allied to the Socialist party organisation. This agreement means thet we should be prevented from defending the best friend of labour on the Liberal side, even if he were a workman, when attacked in an election by a middle-class Socialist endorsed by the executive of the Labour and Socialist party. We are not equal to such a surrender of political liberty and the abandonment in so flagrant a way of common-sense in politics. The arrangement we are asked to submit to is an intolerable one, and the main argument used to justify it—that by it we should purchase safety from attack on ourselves—amounts almost to an insult. We value the honour of a seat in the House of Com- mons highly, but there are things we cannot barter away even to secure that. Further, we venture to point out that most of the members of the trades Union group were returned by Liberal votes, many of them run by Liberal associations. By what right then do these members eater into a compact which not only facilitates the increase of members in the House who denounce Liberalism when- ever they have an opportunity, and also provides for the ultimate extension of working-class representation of Liberalism? We remain convinced of the advan- tages of trade unionism, and are ready at all times to co-operate in advancing its highest interests, and this we believe we are now doing by entering our protest against an agreement which curtails our freedom of political action.—Yours faithfully, lIENRY Vivta A.ND F. 11ADDISON.

YOUTHFUL GAMBLERS.

" A Terrible State of Things."

Numerous cases of gambling were dealt with by the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday. Four boys named James Slater, Chorley-street ; Thomas Toughey, Foundry-street ; Samuel Dickenson, Foundry- street; and James Dickenson, Chapel-court, John-street, were summoned for gaming with cards in Bewsey- street on June Bth. All pleaded guilty. Detective 'Metcalfe said defendants were playing at a game called " banker " on a footpath in Bewsey- street. They were constantly playing there. Slater and Toughey, who had been fined previously for a similar offence, were each find 10s., and the two Dickensons were fined 2s. GI each. Toughey, the two Dickensons, and Edward Jones, Back Dallant-lane, and John Cooper, Greenall-street, were then summoned for gaming with coins, in Qaeen- street, at 11.30 p.m. on Tuesday, June 9th. P.C. Hughes said that at 11.30 on the night of Tuesdaa, June 9th, he saw all the defendants sitting near the Fire Engine Station, playing cards for money. Ile had cautioned them three-quarters of an hour previously. The Chief Constable said it was evident that the parents did not exercise proper control over the boys, by allowing them to be out so late at night. He wished to draw the attention of the Bench to the fact that the offence was committed at 11.30 p.m. The parents of the defendants should be called upon to answer for the conduct of their children. Several of the mothers who were in court were asked to step forward, and they did se, but the excuses they made for not looking after their children were very weak. The Chief Constable said that one of the boys was in a good situation and was doing very well, and it was surprising to find him in such a position that morning. The Mayor said the Bench were quite puzzled what to do in this case. A terrible state of things appeared to exist in Warrington with regard to gambling among boys, and the public of Warrington should be extremely Igrateful to the police for bringing these offenders before the magistrates. The Bench had quite made up their minds to stop this gambling, but it appeared that when r they had got rid of the old gamblers the young ones ' rose up and took their places. A sad feature of the case was that the offence was committed so late at night. He did not know what control the parents had over their children, bat they must surely be aware that they must be itt mischief at such a late hour of the evening. Tougheyand the tvo Dickenson were fined 2s. Gd, each, and Jones and Cooper Is. each. Four boys named Philip Newton, Norman-street ; George Rothman, Norman-street; and James Turner, Turner-street, pleaded guilty to gaming with cards at the rear of Vere-street, on June Bth, and they were each fined 2s. 61. Detective Metcalfe proved the case, and said defend- ants were playing at a game called " banker." Six men named William Hampson, Plumpton-street ; Henry Welsby, Evelyn-street ; Walter Appleton, Plimpton-street; Joseph Darby, Plumpto 7 street ; William Moores, Bostock-street ; and William Jackscn, Hesketh-street, were summoned for gaming with coins at the rear of Plumpton-street, on Sunday, June 7th. All pleaded guilty, with the exception of Appleton, who said he was there, but was not playing. P.C. Clough said he was on duty in company with P.C. Craig, when they saw all the defendants gambling for about half-an-hour. Welsby and Darby were toss- ing coins, and the remainder were betting on them. The language they used was disgraceful. One of the defendants asked Clough how he could " see through a house." P.C. Clough : That is m 7 buelness. I was bidieg and cquld see all that took place. P.C. Craig corroborated. Appleton said he was not playing at all. The MaYea, addressing the defendants, said they ought all to feel ashamed of themselves. Moreover, the offences were committed on a Sunday, and the gambling brought together a crowd of from 50 to 100 people, including boys from 13 to 10 yeers of age, who watched the play. The defendants would be given one more chance. They had only just escaped by two votes of the magistrates a severe punishment, but they might depend upon it that if they were brought up again they would be very heavily fined. They yvotad have to pay les. and costs each.

"SCOTLAND'S DAY"

AT THE SCOTTISH NATIONAL EXHIBITION. Scotland's Day will be celebrated in thoroughly , national manner within the grounds of the Scottish National Exhibition on the 2lth June. The event occurs under the auspices of the Scottish Committee of which Lord Cassillis is Convener. A specially Scottish programme boa been arranged, and it is expected that the day will be made memorable by the gathering of Scotsmen from all over the world. The occasion is to be particularly distinguished by a gathering of Clansmen and it is estimated that some 200 pipers will be present to take part. There will be a Pipe Band contest end a competition in Highland dancing which should prove highly picturesque. An additional source of attraction is the re-appearance of historic groups from Scotland's splendid National Pageant of 13th June. Another noteworthy feature will be the cycle parade, the riders in which will be attired to represent the characters in the novels of Sir Walter Scott. The day will appropriately end with a Scots National concert at which Lowland and Gaelic songs and music will be rendered. Nothing will be wanting to make the event a national success.

LOCAL WEDDINGS,

Nalson-Greenhalgh. The marriage was solemnized at the United Methodist Chard], Castlemere, Rochdale, of Mr. Joseph Standring Nelson, youngest son of Mr. Robert Nelson, wholesale confectioner.. of Sankey-street, Warrington, and Miss Bertha H. There was a pretty wedding at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church on Thursday when Mr. William Thomas Kipps, son of Mr. and Mrs. Kipps, of Colwyn Bay, late of Higher Walton, was married to Miss Florence Booth, daughter of the late Mr. Thomas and Mrs. Booth, of Buttermarket-street. The bride,who was given way by her brother, Mr. A. Booth, was charmingly attired in white silk muslin trimmed with glace silk and chiffon, with hat of tuscart straw trimmed with chiffon.Sbe carried a lovely bouquet of lilies and roses and her ornament was a pearl brooch presented by the bridegroom. She was attended by four bridesmaids—Miss - Hancock (cousin of the bride), Miss Kipps (sister of the bridegroom), Miss Dorothy Brown (niece of the bride), and Miss Mair Williams (niece of the bridegroom). They wore pretty dresses of blue silk eolienne trimmed with silk and net, and their bouquets were composed of pink carnations. Their ornaments we: e pearl and diamond brooch, the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Walter Booth (brother of the bride) discharged the duties of best man. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac.,. presided at the organ and played choice selections including Mendelssohn's "Wedding March." After the ceremony a reception was held at the house of the bride's brother, Mr. Arthur Booth, Stafford-road. Later the happy couple left for South Wales, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a saxe blue costume, with hat to match. ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "The Second Mrs Tariqueray."

The management of this theatre are to be congratu- lated upon providing their patrons with such a high class attraction as Mr. Leonard Pagdon's production of A. W. Pinero's great play " The Second Mrs. i Tanqueray." Although June s not an ideal month for theatre-going, there have been good attendances this week and appreciation of the treat afforded has been most marked. It is but a short time since War- rington playgoers had the opportunity of enjoying another of Mr. Pinero's plays "His House in Order ' 11 which told of the trials of a second wife. In " The Second Mrs. Tanqueray " the author works upon a somewhat similar theme but in an altogether different way, and there is a more sombre note running through the 'play. Mr. Pinero does not even make a concession to the popular desire for a happy ending and the story culminates in tragedy. The plot is so ingeniously woven as to compel one's close attention and the delightful humorous touches are a welcome feature. Such a play indifferently performed would be a dismal failure, but Mr. Leonard Pagden and his company rise to the high demands which it makes and their production is thoroughly adequate and worthy of the highest praise. Mr. Pagden is an admirable Aubrey Tanqueray. There is nothing forced or unnatural about his acting, but not a single point is lost on the audience. He is calm and per- suasive through a long series of trying circumstances but in the last act he gives vent to a fine burst of passion when he curses the man who has blighted his happiness. Miss Lucy Wilson achieves a brilliant success as Paula, fully realizing the varying moods of this complex character At one time she amuses by her studied air of non- chalance and unconcern; then she suddenly flies into a tantrum; and a few minutes later she is making a pathetic appeal for forbearance. The frigid reception which she accords to Lady Cortelyon is quite the most laughable thing in the play. Miss Wilson is a true artiste, and it is no exaggeration to describe her portrayal of Paula as ideal. Mr. J. Edward Pearce makes the most of the part of Cayley Drumunle, the faithful friend whose efforts to restore happiness in the Tanqueray household are attended with such disastrous consequences. He is always genial and imperturbable, yet full of sincerity, and Mr. Pearce's version is wholly acceptable. Miss L-rna Lawrence , is well placed as Ellean, her acting being marked by becoming simplicity and charm. Miss Gladys Bartle- Frere is distinctly successful as Mrs. Cortelyon. Mr. Ernest A. Douglas as Gordon Payne, M.D., and Mr. Montagu Maxey as Frank Misquith, K.C., M.P., take their parts as to the manner born, and Mr. Guy Fletcher does justice to the We of Captain Hugh Ardale. Mr. Jas. Hussey and Miss Muriel Alston as , Sir George and Lady Orreyed are responsible for , considerable amusement.

IRON TRADE COMBINE.

COMPANIES WHICH HAVE JOINED

THE RING.

Local Firm Holds Aloof.

The project for an iron and steel combine appears to be making rapid progress. Some of the leading structural steel and iron engineers in England and Scotland have formed an association of "makers of bridge and nonstructural iron and steel work," designated briefly the B. and C.I.A. To this body the following, amongst other firms, have intimated their adhesion : Messrs. Braithwaite and Kent, West Bromwich. Brandon Bridge Co., Ltd., Motherwell. John Butler and Co., Ltd., Leeds. Cleveland Bridge and Engineering Co., Darlington. Dorman, Long and Co., Middlesborough. P. and M. Maclellan, Ltd., Glasgow. A. and I. Main, Ltd., Glasgow. Somsrvail and Co., Ltd., Glasgow. Toe-side Bridge and Engineering Works, Ltd. Joseph Westwood and Co., Ltd., London. The Widnes Foundry Co., Ltd. Head, Wrightson, and Co., Ltd., Stockton-on-Tees. E. C. and I. Keay, Darlston. T. Horsehay and Co.. Ltd., London. This stage attained, it is proposed that a working agreement between the B. and C.I.A. and the North- East Coast Steelmakera' Association,the Midlands Steel Anglemakers' Association, the Scotch Steelmakers' Association, and the Associated Joist Makers of England and Scotland would be of advantage to all concerned.

As a. basis of agreement it was laid down that to reach success preferential treatment as regards prices and conditions should be extended to the members of the B. and C.I.A. against outstanclers. In return the latter are to place all their contracts for material with those producers who are members of the steel associations which have been named. It is authoritatively stated the committees of the different associations have considered the proposal, and I regarded it with approval, and it is now to be pushed forward vigorously, rho first step intended being to call meetings of those interested in different convenient districts and secure their support. ' The promoters recognise that the project is not free from difficulties, several large houses holding aloof— including Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Limited, who are among the largest firms in the country—but hope is cherished that all obstacles will be removed. Shipbuilding and rail-making are at present outside the scheme, but the former will probably also be incorporated.

WARRINGTON IRON FIRM AND LANCASHIRE COLLIERIES

Following the rumours of big trade amalgamations in all branches there has been published a statement respecting Lancashire collieries and ironworks, which, however, is rather belated. A correspondent of the "Yorkshire Post" states that the whole of the extensive collieries belonging to Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., the Moss Hall Coal Company, Ltd., and Messrs. W. and J. Turner at Abram, Bickershaw, and Platt Zridge, 4ear Wigan, have now been amalgamated, under the title of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, (Ltd.) The capital of Pearson and Knowles is approaching a million. The statement is fairly accurate, but not altogether so, as there has been no amalgamation under one name.

What has happened—and it li'appened about a year ago —is that the directors and others interested in the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Warring- ton, have bought up the shares of the other companies, which continue to be worked and run as separate con- cerns as heretofore. This fact has been mentioned in the reports presented to the annual meetings of the shareholders. The same company have acquired the shares in the great wire-drawing firm of Itylands Bros.' NVarrington, and stress is laid upon the advantage that all the com- panies derive through being customers to one another. Pearson and Knowles have no debentures, and it will be in accord with the known policy of the directors if the Moss Hall debentures are paid off.

Not only does he assutne a different costume for each one, but all his portrayals have appropriate scenic settings which are also changed in a twinkling. We are carried in imagination to the sunny climes of Spain and Italy, and a moment dater we are transported to the Highlands, and Chanti has become a Highlander in the traditional dress and marches on the stage playing the bagpipes. Next he appears as a Russian and indulges in a weird dance, and before the applause which greets this effort has died away ho stands before you as an English gentleman in frock cat and silk hat. The leading lady speaks in the broadest Lancashira but sings with great taste and refinement, the audience being delighted. NON-TRADING IN THE POTTERIES. Tunstall Councillor Disqualified.

- A decision given by the Potteries Stipendiary Magistrate at Tunstall on Thursday disqualified a registrar of births and deaths from acting as a coun- oilier. The decision is one of considerable importance, affecting, it is said, 130 registrars in different parts of the country. Mr. Edward John Oliver, registrar of births and deaths for Tupstall, a member of the Tunstall Council and a supporter of the Potteries federation scheme, was summoned by Mr. George Baker, also a Tunstall councillor, and an opponent of i federation, under the Local Government Act of 1814 for unlawfully acting as a member of tho Council when I disqualified by being concerned in a contract for supply. 'jug the returns of deaths. It appeared from the evidence that the Local Govern meat Board had asked medical officers to distinguish between the deaths of legitimate and illegitimate chil- dren under one sear of age. The medical officer had no means of getting such information except by obtaining the returns from the registrar, The Council therefore made a payment to the defendant of two guineas for these particular returns for the last completed year, and the returns were still being made. The defence was that Mr. Oliver's election did not take place till April 6, and that the contract for these particular returns expired on the last day of March, and no returns of illegitimate births had been sent in since. The Stipendiary Magistrate found that when the defendant was nominated for the Council he was engar,ed in a contract with it. The nomination was therefore bad, and the defendant was not qualified to act on the Council. He fined the defendant a shilling and costs. The finding renders the seat vacant.

The event was one of the most interesting which has ever been decided in Warrington and there were fully 400 specta- tors. As fourteen of the sixteen players left in the closing stages were Warrington men, there were strong hopes that the championship would come here, and Ellison' sncoess proved immensely popular. He had for many years been a clever exponent of the game and is one of the Cemetery Hotel Club's most useful and honoured members.

Mr. J. W. G. Coombs, the deputy president of the Association, in a neat speech, declared the competition open. The spectators in place stood three arid four deep, and the reception accorded to Mr. Coombs was a fitting reward for the services he has rendered through- out the competition. Both these officials have been well backed up by the Warring- ton section. In the closing stages of the competition there were games worth going a long journey to see. Seldom, indeed, have so many strenuous battles been fought at a gathering of Lancashire and Cheshire bowlers.

Cycling.

Those who cycle will often experience a dry and sore throat after a spin along a more or less dusty road, and be at a loss to know how to account for it. This sore-. nes3 is caused for one thing by keeping the mouth open and exposed to the air, thus drying up the natural flow of the saliva, but principally by the myriad grains of dust that clog uri the back of the throat. This is not all ordinary road dust, as those who have examined it !toll us it contains millions of hurtful germs—the bacilli of fever, lockjaw and consumption. Fortunately, how- ever, Nature has provided in the nose a most effectual filter for cleaning the air we breathe. Cyclists and pedestrians would do well to practise breathing through their noses for this reason, as well as another, that breathing through the nose lean infallible preventive of over-exercise and strain. If while doing any exercise you feel you have not enough air without opening your mouth, then that exercise is too much for you, and is straining you unduly.

Jupiter Plavirts seemed to be in one of his generous moods on Saturday last and the heavy showers which he oontiuuerl to bestow during the whole of the after- noon caused an abandonment, of the visit to Hunt's Cross, this proving to be the first check upon what has so far beea a delightful series of successful outings. The faces of the members were imbed a study as they watched and waited for the chance that came not, and they ultimately lesignel themselves to their fate.

Confidence seemed quits restored again when Thou] ty evening turned out on its bast behaviour, and over a dozen members were out for the run to klilton, this being a slight improvement on the last two or three weeks.

V7edne3day evening cause an went without seeing any progress made in the 133wling handicap at Sankey, the weather which svas again vary wet causing this event—which is now in a most interesting state,—to be postponed until next week.

To-dtty the Club ara plying their initial visit of the season into East Cheshire,Studbach being their destina- tion. The visit to this quaint old town Is always greatly enjoyed by the membara and oftener than not is made the medium of a wask-ead run.

Street .Fatality.

At four o'clock on Thursday-afternoon Thomas Buckley, aged two years, son of Alfred Buckley, 28, Knutsford-road, was running across School-street, Latchford, when he was knocked down and run over, it is believed by a horse and lurry. No one saw the accident. The child was immediately picked up and Dr. Langdale was summoned, but on his arrival life was extinct.

Judgment Against Executors.

At the Warrington °aunty CJiirt on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, IC.C., Jolla Aalawaring, auctioneer, Bridge-street, sued John A. Ed yards, Thomas A. Edwards, Alice J. Gaest and Hugh Darbyshire, executors of the late John Edwards, Antrobus, for £l3 16s. for services rendered. Mr. Brow as appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Steel defended. „

No denial was ever made of the debt and no exception .was taken to the amount charged. When the action was entered into Court a notice was flied I intimating that tho Statute of Limitations would be . pleaded in defence. This was signed by Thomas A. I Edwards" on behalf of myself and other executors." One person could not plead for another except by leave I of the Court end he therefore submitted that the notice I was bad and did not bind the Court with regard to other executors.

I —Mr. Steel said the notice was filed by !ill.. Edwards i himself before he (NI r. Stool) took up the case. Ile submitted that the notice was in order as it was not a defence but simply notice that they intended to plead a certain Statute._ It was not a defence. _

His Honour : Is it not ? You surprise me. It is a i defence of the Statute of Limitations and before it . can be pleaded in Court special notice is to be given. ' It must be signed by the person who is going to set up the defence or his authorissd agent—the solicitor. I Mr. Steel : I submit there is no rule to prevent Ole ' defendant giving notice on behalf of himself or others. His Honour : There is no rule authorising it. CHESHIRE EDUCATION.

Medical Inspection Work.

A meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee was hold at Crewe on Monday, Dr. Hodgson presiding. Mr. R. Delaney, of the Khedival Training College, Cairo, who (the chairman stated) had experience in England, India, and Africa of the training of teachers, was appointed principal of the new Cheshire Training College for Teachers at a salary of £5OO per annum. Professor Gunner reported the appointment of Dr. G. Jubb, of Glasgow, and Dr. R. Lawrence, of Black- ball, as assistants to the county medical officer of health, to devote their whole time to the inspection of children in attendance at elementary schools in the 'county elementary education area, at a salary of £350 each per annum and travelling expenses. The Elemen- tary Schools Sub-committee suggested the appointment' also of four nurses, two to work under the direction of each of the doctors. It was thought that one of the nurses should be with each doctor in his examina- tion of the children, and that the other nurse should follow up the cases next day by going to see the parents a children found defective and persuading them to carry out the doctor's instruc- tions. Dr. Hodgson urged that this was most important missionary work. The doctors would be of little use unless their examinations and recommendations were followed up by nurses visiting the children's homes and persuading the parents to get proper remedies for the treatment of defective children. Sufferers from bad eyesight, bad hearing, bad throat, and other physical defects would be examined by the school medical inspector, and much good would result from the atten- tion given to such cases by the nurses. After further discussion, Professor Gunner withdrew his resolution for the appointment of the nurses in order that the subject might be farther considered by the Elementary Schools Committee.

===== 0002601_19080620_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
ORGANISIID GAMES.

Professor Gonne-i-s-aiciih-ai the Elementary Schools Sub-Committee had considered resolutions passel by several of the administrative sub-committtes with reference to provision for organised games, and they now recommended that no further expenditure on organised games should be provided out of the county education rate. lie did not think it would be possible to have organised games uniformly throughout the county. In congested manufacturing districts much might be said in favour of recreation grounds, but be thought they would be largely debarred in such dis- tricts by the expense of providing tliem, while in country districts they were not necessary. lie thought they should wait before going in for further expense with experiments in the direction of organised games. It was decided to discuss the subject at the next meeting.
